This annual report book is respectfully dedicated to the Memory
23
HAROLD D. HARLEY
1891 - 1980
Harold was very active in the affairs of the Town during his entire life and gave many
years of dedicated service. not only to the Planning Board, Conservation Commission
and Town Forest Committee, but also to the entire Town. Harold's recognition of the
importance of land-use planning was visionary. His early achievements in leading
Lunenburg into the adoption of Subdivision Control and Zoning, and the acquisition
of public lands for conservation and park use, will no doubt be honored by the ever-
increasing gratitude of generations of Lunenburgers yet to come.

Board of Selectmen
RUDOLPH MASELLA
ANN P HALL
AUGUST N.MISNER
Board of Selectmen: left to right - Rudolph Masella, Chairman Ann P. Hall, and August N. Misner.
As the new year began and a year of celebration in honor of Lunenburg's 250th birthday came to a close, the
Board of Selectmen looked forward to a return to normalcy in the Selectmen's office. It was not to be.
Tax Cap Fever was upon US 0%?; 4%?; who and what to cut?; can we spend more if the people vote to in-
crease?; how much more? The questions flew around Town Hall, the State House, and everywhere in between. The
problem: Lunenburg's Annual Town Meeting was scheduled to take place long before those on Beacon Hill were ready
to make up their minds. However, under the very capable leadership of Joe Giovino and the Finance Committee,
Town Counsel Bob Larkin, and Town Treasurer Mary Padula, we held our Town Meeting on schedule and managed to
carry law. out the will of the voters in a manner which met all the requirements of the legislation which eventually became
The voters at the Annual Town Meeting made the decision to purchase land andoperate a Town-owned sanitary
landfill as the solution to the serious problem of solid waste disposal. The land has been purchased, the preliminary
plans approved, and assignment of the land was made by the Board of Health after a public hearing in December.
Final plans are being prepared by the engineer; George Munyon, Highway Superintendent, has been named Opera-
tions Manager; and the Board fully expects to open the new facility next summer and simultaneously to begin closure
procedures on the facility on Townsend Harbor Road.
The Annual Town Meeting of 1979 was notable in at least two other respects as well. First, attendance at the
meeting was the highest in recent memory: 808 according to the official tally by the Town Clerk. Last but no
means least was the retirement from public office of one of Lunenburg's most dedicated and tirelesspublic servants --
Sherwood Winchester. After thirty-two years of service as Forest Warden, Police Chief, Fire Chief, Constable, and
- 4 -
===PAGE 7===
Selectman, "Winch" is missed around Town Hall. However, he is still verymuch to be seen around Town behind the
wheel of his school bus, and his big smile is always a welcome sight.
May and June were quiet as new Selectman Gus Misner joined the Board in carrying out the routine duties of
the office.
Then came JULY! "Contaminated Wells, Don't Drink the Water!", said the State.
Then came AUGUST! "Laboratory Error", said EPA.
The summer of '79 will long be remembered in Lunenburg as the summer of the "water problem". The Town
struggled through five very long weeksof a nightmare involving an effort to find the answer to questions about what
had happened as the result of the illegal dumping of chemicals in a sand pit off Townsend Harbor Road. Hickory
Hills Lake was threatened, as well as all the private wells around the lake and all the way to the Shirley town line.
We learned that medical science did not know what effects consumption of the chemicals supposedly involved would
have on the human body. We learned that there was no established procedure to provide potable water to homes
deprived of the use of their wells by contamination. We learned there was no established procedure for cleaning up
a chemical spill which obviously needed to be cleaned up before it spread further. We learned that financial assist-
ance for even such an immense disaster was by no means assured. No one had the answers to the hard questions we
had to ask. However, many people did make an extra effort in what was a time of crisis for Lunenburg and the fol-
lowing are deserving of a vote of thanks from US all: All Town departments, the Lunenburg Water District Commiss-
ioners and staff, Mr. Kendall Crocker of Ashby, Mayor David Gilmartin of Fitchburg, all our elected legislators
both state and federal, and lastly the many individuals who as members of the oft maligned bureaucracy made an
extra effort to assist US in whatever ways they were able.
On August 16th at a meeting arranged by Congressman Drinan to bring together all the federal agencies who
might be able to help the Town, an abrupt change in direction took place. Dr. Thomas Spittler of the Environmental
Protection Agency announced that previous tests conducted by the state laboratory on Lunenburg wells were "erro-
neous", and that the warning of contamination of drinking water was based on false reports due to laboratory error.
The Department of Environmental Quality Engineering is currently supervising a hydrogeological study at the
site of the chemical dump; and until the Board receives some definite report on the results of that study, including
the extent of the contamination and probable future impacton the area, no specific actionis planned. It is possible
to report that testing of selected wells is continuing by DEQE and that all tests to date have shown the water to be
safe for drinking purposes at this time.
September found the Board doing the work necessary to submit a Pre-application to HUD for a Community Dev-
elopment Block Grant to install water mains in the Townsend Harbor Road-Hemlock Drive area. The grant was sub-
sequently denied.
November brought a Special Town Meeting to deal with the recommended purchase of salt spreaders which
would reduce the use of salt on our roads in winter icing conditions. The new awareness of the precious and pre-
cariousness nature of water quality made this a popular issue and the spreaders have been purchased. Also at this
time-omen of what is to come additional funds were to various accounts because of the unprecedented
rise in the cost of oil and gasoline.
In December the Board and the Police Union reached agreement on a new contract after many months of ne-
gotiations. Also, preliminary budget meetings were held with major department heads.
It is plain that the closing year of the seventies was a precursor to the eighties and the problems we are all to
face in the next decade the danger to drinking water supplies was vividly experienced here at home; with mixed
horror and apprehension we watch the news from the Mid-East especially focusing on Iran and Afghanistan; the spi-
raling cost of oil and the realization that the supply is finite at any cost; the near miss at Three Mile Island. As we
prepare both Town and family budgets for the coming year and wonder what path lies ahead, we are again reminded
that although the aforementioned problems are national in scope, they are very personal in effect; and it is each of
US singly and together in every city and town across the nation who are the United States of America. To paraphrase
Robert Frost, we have miles to go before we sleep, miles to go before we sleep.

The Boardof Selectmen, as the Licensing Authority, granted or renewed the following licenses: 15 for the sale
of alcoholic beyerages and 27 special one-day licenses for the sale of alcoholic beverages, 39 licenses relating to
Whalom Amusement Park, 23 common victualer licenses and approximately 65 miscellaneous licenses.
As 1979 drew to a close, and after many hours of negotiation sessions, final arrangements were in process for
the signing of the Collective Bargaining Agreement between the Town and Local #353 of the International Brother-
hood of Police Officers,
Shown at Contract signing are: Seated left to right - R. Masella, Selectman; A. Hall, Chairman of Selectmen;
M. D'Aguanno, Union Negotiator. Standing left to right - F. Hobbs, Police Chief; R. Eley, Executive Secretary
to Selectmen; R. Fitzgerald, Police Sergeant.
Board of Health
The following is a brief resume of services receivedby our Town through Nashoba Associated Boards of Health:
In the Nashoba Nursing Service, the term "health promotion" is used to describe activities that relate to dis-
ease prevention. As this term implies, the Nashoba Community Health Nurses, such as Mrs. Shirley Carpenter, work
to promote the highest level of health for the citizens of the community. Some examples of these activities include:
home visits to expectant mothers, to newborns, especially those of high risks; to elderly citizens requiring health coun-
seling or assistance from available community resources; and to the chronically ill in need of assistance. Other ac-
tivities include screening for early detection of disease and communicable disease investigation and control. The
Nursing Service also serves as the Certified Home Health Agency (VNA) for 14 Nashoba towns.
Nashoba's Nursing Service also provides home care services in conjunction with the area's home care corpora-
tions (the agencies serving the needs of the elderly), with the statewide Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS) Pro-
gram, and the State's Lead Poisoning Project. Nursing Service activities during 1979 were: Nursing Visits, Health
Promotion - 287; Nursing Visits, Disease-Related - 986; Physical Therapy Visits - 47; Home Health Aide Visits - 889
(hours expended in Home Health Aide Visits were 2,006). Also, 28 Well Adult and Oldster Clinics were held in
Lunenburg attended by 723 persons.
One of the least obvious, but most important prevention services by Nashoba is in the area of Environmental
Health. This Division is responsible for the prevention of disease and injury in the areas of: food service, such as
markets and restaurants; housing; recreational facilities, such as camps, beaches and swimming pools; sewage disposal;
wells; public water supplies; nuisance conditions. An additional benefit is in the improvement of environmental
quality. In 1979, some of the Environmental Health Services included: Food Service Inspections - 14; School Sur-
veys - 7; Well Inspections/Samples - 167; Bathing Beach Inspections/Samples - 52; Nuisance Investigations - 40;
Housing Inspections - 4; Sewage Disposal System Inspections - 302; Sewage Disposal System Permits - 33.

Planning Board
No->
we
tows
Planning Board: Seated left to right - Chairman Patrick J. Slattery and F. Clifton Miller.
Standing left to right - Robert J. Ebersole, Charles H. B. Arning and David P. Demers.
In the continuing process of working to maintain a logical, moderate growth pattern for the Town, the Planning
Board has concluded another intense year of work. Projects for the year ranged from the approval of a new eleven
lot subdivision off Arbor Street, to the bi-weekly process of approving new lots not requiring subdivision approval, to
our current task of reviewing the sequence of updating the Town's Master Plan, last prepared twenty years ago.
Currently, areas of greatest concern are being pinpointed for study and consultants are being reviewed to en-
able the Planning Board to present to the Town a proposal to prepare a new Master Plan which will address the future
effect of areas such as:
(a) water quality and use.
(b) the extension of I-190 and its effect on the Town.
(c) the impact on Town services of extended or accelerated growth.
As this process continues, the Planning Boardwill be seeking large amounts of input from the Town Boards, Commis-
sions and its citizens to insure a complete and well rounded plan with direction.
This year the Planning Board accepted with regret the resignation of Ronald D. Colvin. This vacancy was
filledby Charles H. B. Arning who was selected by the unanimous joint vote of the Board of Selectmen and Planning
Board to fill out the year's term.

===PAGE 10===
Board of Assessors
Boardof Assessors: left to right - Secretary Ine 1. Pihlaja, William F.
Reed, Kathryn M. Herrick and Chairman Bernard J. Paton.
On January 1, 1980 your Boardof Assessors will increase all valuations by 10%. This increase will include all
land, real estate and commercial property in the Town. This action is necessary to comply with State law, that all
property must be valued at full and fair cash value. This is part of a program started in 1975 by your Board of As-
sessors to maintain the revaluation of the Town done in 1969 and 1970 by the firm of Whipple, Magane and Darcy.
When the new revaluation was put into effect in 1970, the Town was at 100% full and fair cash value. The Assessors
were given guidelines by the revaluation firm for the purpose of maintaining the new system. Each year from Sep-
tember to March, we view every piece of property in Town and record any changes made. By following these prac-
tices, we have been able to maintain accurate data on all property in Town. When the State Department of Cor-
porations and Taxation informed US in 1975 that Lunenburg (due to rapidly increasing Real Estate values) wasno longer
at full and fair value and must submit a plan to the State Department of Corporations and Taxation on how we were
going to correct this situation or face court action, we were able to submit the following plan that was approved by
the State.
1. The Town had been revalued in 1969 and 1970 by the firm of Whipple, Magane and Darcy.
2. The Assessors and staff were thoroughly instructed in the operation and use of the Whipple, Magane and
Darcy evaluation and assessment system. This included field work and record keeping. We were left with
the firm's manuals, guides and forms to maintain the system in equity.
3. All property is viewed on a yearly basis to insure up-to-date detailed drawings on property record cards.
4. All assessing courses offered by the Commonwealth of Massachusetts are attended by all Assessors and clerks
to maintain knowledge of the law.
5. values. Statistical study of all sales since 1970 has been maintained and will be used as a base year for established
- 8 -
===PAGE 11===
Wehave sales broken down to establish the direction of sale prices and the average percentage increase over-
all for a given year plus the same information on specific types of houses. The last information is necessary to deter-
mine value movement within atype house, A secondstudy was set up consisting of a selection of homes in each spe-
cific type and revaluation of these homes using the new 1974 Department Property Assessment Manual system and the
manual figures.
Upon completion of this project the Board of Assessors have hard data to determine exact percentage increases
required to modify existing assessed valuation of real estate. (It might be brought out that a new valuation at this
time could cost between $50,000 and $60,000. Updating as the Town is doing, by an outside firm, could cost over
$19,000.)
The Board of Assessors increased all property in Town 10% in 1976 and another 10% in 1977. However, due to
a slow down in construction and uncertain economic conditions, we didnot increase valuations in 1978 or 1979. Now
as we enter 1980 and check the real estate sales for the past year, your Assessors find that we have property valued
at only 60% of full market value. Therefore, we find it absolutely necessary to increase all valuations in Town by
another 10% to stay within the guidelines set down by the State Supreme Court that all property must be valued at
full and fair market value. This action will have the effect of increasing the total valuation of the Town by more
than seven million dollars, however, it will also lower the tax rate if the Town budgets were to remain the same as
last year and the total amount of money a person would pay for taxes would also remain the same.
In 1980 your Assessors find there is a new law on the books called classification. This law divides the Town
into four different classes for taxation and allows each class to be taxed at a different rate. The four classes are as
follows: Homeowners, Industrial, Commercial and Open Spaces. The Town will be required to increase the valua-
tions of all property to 100% full and fair market value and then have this valuation certified by the State. At this
time with the advice and consent of the Selectmen, the reduced classification values will be instituted and values will
be maintained by factoring every two years on the advice of the State Department of Revenues. Another new law re-
quires Assessors to undertake studies sponsored by the State and pass examinations which will assure some standard of
competence of individual Assessors and standard assessing practices throughout the Commonwealth. Your Assessors
have three years to comply with this new law, and only certified Assessors will be allowed to take office in the fu-
ture.
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that have been enacted concerning the
value of residential property, owned and occupied as a domicile by persons in various groups for whom the payment
of taxes could constitute an undue burden. These exemptions may not exceed specific amounts. If a taxpayer feels
that he qualifies for any of the following exemptions, he should contact the Assessors' Office and request an appli-
cation which lists all the requirements that must be met in order to qualify.
Section 17
- Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation or atax
of $175, whichever is greater.
Section 22
- Disabledor wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000 valuation
or a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of disability.
Section 22E
- Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100% disability.
Section 37
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section 41
- Persons aged 70 or over $4,000 valuation or a tax of $500, whichever is greater.
Section 41A
- Tax deferral for certain persons65 years of ageor over with gross receiptsof under $20,000 an-
nually to place the taxes on his domicile and one acre of land on a deferred payment plan, the
taxes to be paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Section 42
- Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation
or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43
- Minor orphans of policeofficers or firefighters killed in the line of duty. $8,000 valuation or a
tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 61A
Section 9
- Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its agricultural
or horticultural use.
Chapter 61
Section 1-6
- Provides for assessment of forest land managed under a planned program and certification of a
State Forester.

===PAGE 12===
Library Trustees
Library Trustees: Seated left to right - Janet M. Cragin, Beverly A. Mulholland, Chairman Barbara T. Murray and
June Dettenrieder. Standing left to right - Librarian John E. Ramsay, Richard F. Beardmore, Jr. and John J. Boursy.
What can you buy today for $7.61? Two movie tickets, four pounds of hamburger, seven gallons of gasoline
or the complete range of services of the Ritter Memorial Library, which includes borrowing books, records, au-
diovisual materials and equipment, usingreference sources and the help of the Library staff, the photocopier, meet-
ing room and children's room, and enjoying many kinds of activities and programs. The Library budget, about one
percent of the total Town budget, is the best investment per capita that Lunenburg residents made in 1979.
Adults borrowed 18, 473 books (86 on interlibrary loan), 599 records, 1, 291 magazines, 68 pamphlets, and 58
regional library system films, for a total of 20,489 items. Children tookout 19,986 books (4,268 to the children at
the Primary School), 77 magazines, 18 puzzles, 152 filmstrip kits, and 384 records for a sum of 20,617. The grand
total for materials borrowed was 41, 106. If there were no library service available here, each of US would have had
to pay $440 for these materials at today's prices, instead of the $7.61 we actually spent!
Because the price of books has risen so high, the Library has adopted a "get tough" policy for retrieving long
overdue materials. After a well-publicized amnesty period last spring, a blitz of phone calls, notices, and certi-
fied letters resulted in 99 of 129 items either being returned or paid for a savings of almost $700. This retrieval
policy will continue, resorting to court action if necessary, to prevent the loss of expensive Town resources and to
keep all materials available for your use.
Wehave recently completed another important economizing measure a three-year program to make our sev-
enty year old building more energy efficient. This effort has involved tuning and readjusting the heating system,
lowering thermostat settings, and installing storm windows on all three floors. These actions should translate into
fewer dollars being wasted on ever-increasing fuel and utility costs.
- 10 -
===PAGE 13===
Most Library activities are not measurable in dollars spentor saved, but do contribute a great deal to the value
received for your tax dollars. The Library began "Project Alert" this year to assure that despite our limited supply
of books, Lunenburg students can be sure to obtain the materials they need to fulfill their class assignments. To avoid
having just a few students rush in to the Library as soon as a general project is announced and check out all books on
the topic, we have asked teachers to fill out a card alerting the Library in advance of their projects. The Library
can then set aside all relevant materials and make sure that no one takes out too many. In this way, although our
circulation figures may suffer, our resources are available to all who need them.
The Library's meeting room, which is a new resource, has attracted many community groups in 1979. in our
Town, with no other public building regularly open after 5:00 P.M., this facility has provided space for public meet-
ings of the Community Council, 4-H Club, Historical Society, Scholarship Committee, Council on Aging, Common
Cause, Lunenburg's Class of '82, Friends of the Library, Central Mass. Regional Library System and the Alumni As -
sociation. Even the Library's photocopier received record use this year, with 10,778 copies being made by patrons.
Having this service in the Center of Town saves traveling to Fitchburg for this convenience. In the coming year, we
would like to obtain a better quality plain paper copier to improve this service for our users.
As usual, children participated enthusiastically in the programs designed for them. Songfests, craft workshops,
movies, the Children's Art Festival, and Preschool Story Hours all offered them enjoyable encounters with the Lib-
rary and its resources. The summer reading program, "Hello World", resulted in 1,867 books being read, with ten
children reading over sixty books, and two of these finishing 85 and 98 books. Activities included two movies, a
T-shirt workshop, a musical revue, a magic show, an animal exhibit, and a puppet show.
Two special programs provided both education and enjoyment for our residents. The Friends of the Library's
Interest Series, which is in its fifth year, offered programs on crafts, genealogy, women's issues, taxes, diet and an-
tiques. Emily Foster, Children's Librarian, and Friend of the Library, Lorraine Mendoza, collaborated to bring our
residents more International Nights, among them Bavaria by Beatrice Adams, Yugoslavia by Elbridge & Ruth Hoag,
China by Mr. & Mrs. Stanley Chittick of Fitchburg, and India by Mr. & Mrs. Hemendra Shah.
It is unusual for a Library in a Town the size of Lunenburg to offer such a variety of services andsuch economi-
cal management of its resources. It is a tribute to the efforts of our Friends of the Library andour Professional Lib-
rary staff: Elizabeth Burnap, Emily Foster and Nancy Bigelow. Our Librarian for nearly six years, John Ramsay, is
leaving in March to become Director of the Auburn, Massachusetts Library, but the standards he has set for pro-
fessional service will remain,
Without doubt, the $7.61 each of US spends on the Ritter Memorial Library is money well spent. Where else
can you get so much for so little?
S
Park Commission
The 1979 Summer Recreation Program was ahuge success again this year under the leadership of our Director,
Richard Stachowicz. He was ably assisted by Karen Lindsay at the Wallis Park Playground and Cindy St. Arnaud at
the Central Playground. Both of these playgrounds were well attended because of the quality of work and enthusiasm
of both instructors. We should also not fail to mention the fine Arts & Crafts Program under the direction of Joan
Carey and Chris Leary.
Men's Softball had another good year with R. A. Powell and Web Nik winning their respective divisions, and
R. A. Powell winning the Playoff Championship. The only drawback was that we had to use Marshall Park twice a
week because of lack of available playing space. The Commissioners are working to resolve this problemby proposing
the development of property off West Street.
Junior League Street Hockey and Basketball ran very smoothly under the direction of Chris Whitty. The Sum-
mer High School Basketball Program was also very successful. St. Bernard's and Leominster won their respective div-
isions, and St. Bernard's won the League Championship by beating Leominster by five points.
- 11 -
===PAGE 14===
The Girl's Softball Programs were again successful thanks to Chuck Wennerberg. The Women's Soccer Program
was under the direction of Rick Hens. This was a new program started last year, and should this program be included
in next year's program, it will be given more publicity.
The Summer Recreation Program concluded another year with its Fourteenth Annual Muscular Dystrophy fund-
raiser. A total of $312.65 was netted for this most worthy cause. The Town Beach was ably directed by Jerry Beloin.
The Commissioners were pleased with the success of the swim instruction programs.
Jack Therrien instructed the Tennis Program in our Town. The attendance of 107 persons is broken down as
follows: 9 to 13 age group, 58; high school age group, 17; Whalom group, 18; and adult group, 14. The Commis-
sioners are aware of the conditions of the tennis courts and are working on plans to repair same.
A new plan has been undertaken called Life Course. It consists of a series of twenty exercise stations built
along a jogging path or track. It is designed to improve endurance, strength and flexibility for those who use the
course. It is open to all ages. The Rotary Club gave $500 towards the program. The Park Commission will be seek-
ing additional funds from individuals and businesses for the construction of the stations. The course at Marshall Park
will be approximately three-quarters of a mile in length. It is expected to be completed by early spring. Mr. Brian
Roberts deserves a lot of credit for the initiation of this program.
Our Commission would like to thank all who in any way helped to make our programs so successful. Thanks
to Karen Reed for help in our office. Special thanks to Town Clerk, Mary Padula, and her able staff. A very spe-
cial thank you to our Supervisor, Bob Garneau, and his able assistant, Chuck Letarte, who kept our facilities in
excellent condition. We have heard many comments about the good job being done on fixing up the fields. The
Park Commissioners are sad to report that Bob Garneau, who has given so many years of dedicated service to our
parks and grounds, is to retire as of January 1980. We will surely miss his expertise and his dedication to the Town
of Lunenburg. He took so much pride in the oppearance of our parks.
In closing, the Commissioners would like to appeal to the townspeople to support US at the forthcoming Annual
Town Meeting.

Housing Authority
Housing Authority: Seated left to right - Chairman Josephine C. Meloon, George E. Webster, Jr. and
Ella Rae Denault. Standing left to right - Executive Secretary Eileen M. Alleva and Richard I. White.
The Lunenburg Housing Authority is in its tenth year in the operation of the facilities at 131 White Street
known as the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly. Presently, we have forty-three apartments occupied by single
persons and five apartments occupied by couples.
The tenants have their own organized Pearl Brook Tenants' Association which meets on a regular basis and
arranges interesting programs and projects for the benefit and enjoyment of its members. Mrs. Dorothy Williams
holds the office of President and is the principal liaison between the tenants and the Authority members.
The requirements for occupancy are that applicants be at least 65 years of age and in need of good housing.
Our waiting list of applicants continues to grow. Rents are variable in that no more than 25% of income canbe
charged for rent which includes heat, water and electricity. The Authority feels the rising cost of energy with the
electric heat for our forty-eight apartments, and is finding it very difficult to keep within our budget.
Our tenants are provided with transportation two days a week for their personal needs. The shut-ins are pro-
vided with the "Meals on Wheels". The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health provides the Well Oldster Clinics
monthly for our elderly. Home Health Care provides household duties and shopping for those not able to perform
these tasks for themselves.
The tenantsof the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly participate in and enjoy the many interesting activities
held throughout the year and also take an active part in community affairs.
The Housing Authority invites anyone who is interested to inquire at the Housing Authority office where Mrs.
Eileen Alleva, Executive Secretary, will be available to answer questions.

Tree Warden
One hundred nine diseased trees were cut, chipped
and buried. The Department of Natural Resources gave
five days ($700.00) of tree removal work and two days of
tagging trees suspected of having Dutch Elm Disease.
These samples were sent to the University of Massach-
usetts for analysis. The Town was reimbursed $497.42
by the County. A total of $9,204.00 was spent under
the Dutch Elm Account.
Work under the supervision of the Tree Warden
amounted to $6,704.46. A brush chipping program was
initiated this year whereby townspeople could bring their
brush to the Town garage and have it chipped. All brush
had to be freshly cut, no debrisor oldbranches. No brush
whatsoever was accepted from any contractors. This pro-
gram was greatly appreciated by Lunenburg residents, in
view of the fact that the Municipal Refuse Disposal Area
does not accept brush.
The Tree Warden appreciates the cooperation and
Richard W. Brown, Tree Worden
assistance he received throughout the year.

Historical Commission
The newly formed Historical Commissionheld its first meeting in June for the purpose of undertaking a survey
of the architecturally and historically significant areas, buildings, monuments, archeological sites, cemeteries and
structures in Town. They met with Liz Durfee, Inventory Coordinator for the Massachusetts Historical Commission,
to discuss the functions and survey process of the local Commission. Presently, the Commission is photographing,
researching and processing information relating to the Center of Town and the surrounding buildings. The following
is an excerpt from a booklet on Historical Commissions published by Office of the Secretary of the Commonwealth,
PURPOSE OF A LOCAL HISTORICAL COMMISSION
The local historical commission is the agency responsible for identifying and recording the historic assets
of its community and for developing and implementing a program for their preservation. The commission
cooperates withother municipal agencies such as the planning board and conservation commission to in-
sure that the goals of historic preservation are considered in planning for future development of the com-
munity. The commission also coordinates the activities of local groups whose interests involve history
or historic preservation, such as local historical societies and private preservation organizations, serving
as the liaison between these groups and the state, and Federal agencies and preservation organizations.
The inventory, which is the primary responsibility of the local historical commission, provides the basis
for all preservation planning in the community. It provides the Massachusetts Historical Commission
with the information necessary to assist communities in protecting historic properties from adverse effect
caused by publicly funded or licensed projects. It also provides the basis for evaluation by both the
local historical commission and the Massachusetts Historical Commission of properties suggested for nom-
ination to the National Register of Historic Places. The Massachusetts Historical Commission receives
National Register suggestions submitted by the local historical commission based on a locally conducted
inventory of the community's historic assets. The State commission seeks the advise of the historical com-
mission in local allocation of Federal historic preservation grants administered by the Massachusetts His-
torical Commission. For these reasons it is important for each city and town to have such a Commission.
Historical Commission: Seated left to right - Jean E. Hobbs, Paul B. Porter and Chairman Janice P.
Carrier. Standing left to right - William R. Proctor, Jr., Glenn S. Stillman and Raymond A. Powell Jr.

===PAGE 19===
Personnel Committee
The Town of Lunenburg is fortunate in having in its employ many extremely conscientious and capable people,
at all levels. They make a significant contribution to the quality of life in our Town, It has been and is the inten-
tion of the Personnel Committee to deal fairly with these people. However, the Committee must also work within
the framework of what is fair to the taxpayers of Lunenburg, upon whom falls the burden of paying the salaries of
Town employees,
Over the past several years, the Committee has felt obliged to limit salary increases for Town employees, so
as not to place an unfair burden on the taxpayers when they could ill afford it. This has, of course, resulted in a
falling-behind of wage levels in the Town. The Committee feels that this year, something should be done to bring
things back into line. We are, therefore, proposing a somewhat larger than usual percentage adjustment in the
overall Salary Schedule. To compensate for this, our Department Heads have been asked to curtail other increases
wherever possible, so that the adjustment can be made without inflating the total budgets more than is absolutely
necessary.
Aside from the overall percentage increase, the amended Classification and Salary Schedule reflects a nec-
essary increase in the mileage allowance, from 12c to 17c per mile, and a small number of adjustments to particular
salary categories, where inequities were brought to the Committee's attention. We feel that the resultant proposed
plan is as equitable as we can make it, both for the employees and for the taxpayers who will be passing judgment
on it at the coming Annual Town Meeting.
Personnel Committee: Seated left to right - Lynn F. Markham, Chairman Anne M. Davis
and Beatrice E. Stepton. Standing left to right - John L. Plank, Jr. and Paul F. Gelinas .

===PAGE 20===
Conservation Commission
The Conservation Commission met twenty-three times in regular and special meetings during 1979. Six public
hearings were held on thirteen requests for determination of applicability to the Wetlands Act. This means that out
of twenty-three meetings, nineteen were spent primarily on the Wetlands Act. This activity is the result of the
dwindling number of good building lots. This increases the demand for marginal lots most of which are considered
wet lots. The building of homes on wet lots located in the watershed serving the Water District is a distinct threat
to the quality of our drinking water. Vigilance by the Board of Health, Planning Board and Conservation Commission
will ensure that our water supplies are not endangered by helter-skelter building in the watershed area.
The Conservation Commission strongly endorses the purchase of the development rights of the so-called Levin
Farm at the intersection of Massachusetts Avenue and Electric Avenue. This 175-acre tract would then remain ag-
ricultural land from this time on. The enormous amount of Massachusetts farm land given up for development will
hurt US in the future. If we act now in unison with the state "Agricultural Preservation Act" this prime agricultural
property will be saved from development.
In Memory
As 1980 began, a valiant fighter for the cause of conservation quietly faded from
the scene. A few hours after being stricken at hishome on Sunnyhill Road, Harold Harley
passed away on January 3, 1980.
He will be sorely missed by those of US who worked with him on the Conservation
Commission. The wisdom of his counsel, the sharpness of his wit, and his selfless dedi-
cation to the Commission and its work will long be remembered by those who follow in
his footsteps.
Footsteps that began before conservation became fashionable. Footsteps that
bore the vision of Marshall Park, Marshall Pond, Lesure Park, the Town Forests, the
Cowdrey Nature Center and Laurelbank Farm. Footsteps that carried the Town into sub-
division control and zoning laws that protected the Town from over development and
helped maintain its pastoral beauty.
The footsteps have stopped, but thanks to Harold Harley the roadway to conserva-
tion is broad and inviting to those who want to keep Lunenburg a beautiful Town to
live in.
S
Town Forest Committee
my
The Town Forest Committee met on an average of at least once a month during the past year. Our Chairman,
Harold D. Harley, suggested that the boundary lines on all Town Forest land and Conservation land should be sur-
veyed and plainly marked. However, our budget does not allow this expenditure. The road into the Town Forest
off Gilchrest Street was leveled to make it more passable. The southerly area of the Town Forest off Gilchrest
Street, where the laurel display is located, was surveyed and the property line marked.
We try to keep the roads, paths and trails passable each year. There are some wonderful and unique areas in
our Town Forests. Don't forget that our natural resources are our most valuable asset.
The Town Forest Committee was deeply saddened by the death of our long-time member, Harold D. Harley,
in early January of 1980, Harold will be greatly missed by the Committee.

Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee
In view of its findings that sewerage and drainage problems do exist in certain areas of Town, the Sewerage
and Drainage Investigative Committee recommended to the 1979 Annual Meeting that $18,000 be appropriated to
match a Federal EPA Grant of $54,000. The $72,000 is to be used to engage an engineering firm to make a detailed
study to update the 1973 Morgenroth Report by identifying existing problem areas, determining future needs, and to
prepare a plan for reasonable, more cost effective alternatives to an extensive sewer system and treatment plant.
Following a favorable vote under Article 13 of the April 7, 1979 Annual Meeting Warrant, this Committee
interviewed engineering firms and selected Dufresne-Henry Engineering Corp. of Springfield, Vermont to prepare
an engineering service contract for the Step I Wastewater Facilities Planning work and to prepare an application for
funding to the Massachusetts Division of Water Pollution Control and request that the United States Environmental
Protection Agency approve the scope of services fees. Although it was agreed that upon approval and award of the
Grant by EPA the Town would finalize a contract with Dufresne-Henry, it was also agreed that in the event the EPA
Grant should not be received, the Town would have incurred no financial liability.
The agreement with Dufresne-Henry was executed on May 21, 1979, and Step I Facilities Plan wassubmitted
to the State on July 31, 1979, followed by supplementary information submitted on October 12, 1979. Finally, on
January 23, 1980, a Staff Engineer from the Division of Water Pollution Control contacted the engineers and in-
dicated that except for a few minor changes, the plans met with the State's approval. On January 28, 1980, the
engineers were provided with an outline of the required minor changes, the changes have been made, and the plans
and completed grant applications will have been executed and submitted to the State and EPA by the time you read
this report. Although the preliminary State review took longer than anticipated, Dufresne-Henry is of theopinion
that the Project can still commence in the Spring of 1980, in accordance with the original timetable.
Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee: left to right - David D. Blake, Chester J.
Pliska, Chairman Bernard M. Freeman, William J. Kearney and Mary L. Padula.
Town By-Law Committee
The Town By-law Committee was established in accordance with Article 7 of the 1977 Annual Town Meeting.
Per vote of the Town under said Article 7, the Board of Selectmen and Moderator appointed five members who were
voters at large in the Town to serve on this Committee for the purpose of reviewing, updating and recommending to
the Town changes to the Town By-laws established pursuant to Section 21 of Chapter 40 of the General Laws. The
Committee has met on numerous occasions to review and comment on the present Town By-laws. During 1980,
the Committee will present for review and consideration certain changes and recommendations to the Town By-laws.

Council on Aging
The Councilon Aging proudly presents the accomplishments of 1979. The "Meals on Wheels" Program, now in
its fourth year of serving the elderly shut-ins, has proven to be a successful venture. From January through Decem-
berof this year, 2,972 meals have been delivered by our very dedicated volunteers who have traveled 6, 598 miles.
We, on the Council, are delighted to be able to fulfill the needs of the elderly shut-ins with this program. We feel
the need for this program is ever-increasing and will do our very best to continue this most worthwhile effort. This
program has been solely successful through our faithful volunteers, the School Lunch Personnel, Montachusett Nu-
trition Program, and our Public Health Nurse.
The "Transportation for the Elderly" has proven to be an added and most beneficial program. We are still of-
fering this service on Tuesdays and Thursdays from 9 A.M. to 1 P.M. for medical, dental, banking, shopping and
personal needs. With the increased cost of gasoline, insurance, etc., we feel that the need is certainly growing for
this service. We are most grateful to Einard Warpulafor such excellent service since the institution of the program.
Through the fine efforts of Council Member, Marcella Peed, and her committee of volunteers along with the
cooperation of the United Parish Building Committee, the Senior Citizen Drop-In Center made its debut starting in
April. The Drop-In Center is open every Wednesday at the United Parish Building from 10 A.M. to 1 P.M. Senior
Citizens are welcome to drop in for a donut and a cup of coffee, join their friends and make new ones, and just so-'
cialize. Since the initial opening, enthusiasm has grown, and the Council is pleased to see such a great response to
a long awaited need. It is the anticipation of the Council to acquire the facilities of a permanent Drop-In Center
at some future date.
The Seventh Annual Christmas Dinner held at the High School was attended by 175 Senior Citizens, as well as
several Council members. A delicious meal was prepared by Mrs. Marjorie Stillman and her personnel. Entertain -
ment was provided under the direction of Donald Adams and Donald Loudmaking everyone present forget that it was
snowing.
The Council welcomes the addition of Dorothy Paradis to serve with us, and we are certainly most apprecia-
tive of her well coordinated program of volunteers for our "Meals on Wheels". This past year the Council regrets
the loss of Barbara Jordan who has moved out of Town. Barbara's untiring efforts will be sorely missed.
Councilon Aging: Seated left to right - Elvira z. May, Doris M. Carrier, Lucienne Chapdelaine,
Dorothy Paradis and Chairman Martha L. Molaghan. Standing left to right - Mark G. Thatcher,
Reverend John E. Sandberg, Milton C. Carpenter, Richard F. Beardmore, Jr. and Alfred C. Gil-
chrest. Absent when picture was taken were Arthur J. Brockelman and Marcella W. Peed.

Highway Department
How many of you remember those rows of 5 or
6 red and white verse signs posted along the road-
side quoting humorous messages? Let me jog your
memory: "He saw the train
and tried to duck it
he kicked the gas
and then the bucket".
Burma-Shave. These signs spread like summertime
dandelion fluff across the nation. The row of signs
were placed 300 feet apart and at 35 mph it took
about three seconds from sign-to-sign or about 20
seconds to pass the entire series.
Things were different in the "roaring twen-
ties". In May 1927, the last Model T Ford came
off the production line, having lost some of its pop-
ularity to the disc-wheeled Chevy. The Model A
was unveiled December 2, 1927, when thousands
waited patiently outside showrooms to get glimpses
of this new wonder with such refinements as wind-
shield wings, four-wheel brakes and colors other
than black. These were the formative years of our
love affair with automobiles.
Gasoline and oil were usually dispensed by
local stores or repair shops. Tall red gas pumps
stood proudly in front of the establishment, topped
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
by a glass cylinder calibrated in gallons. Gas was
pumped by hand into the glass and then flowed by
gravity into the tank. One watched the merchant
very closely to sure he didn't pump "short". Gas was selling for 20 cents per gallon; oil was pumped from a drum
into quart measures at 10 cents. Some stores and shops carried a premium grade oil at 15 cents per quart.
In 1920 the first white lines were painted down the center of a road in Wayne County, Michigan. The idea
soon spread across the country, along with yellow lines showing that it was unsafe to pass. Stop signs, which told
drivers who should wait and who should go, began to appear at crossroads. Automatic traffic signals first appeared
in Detroit in the early 1920's.
All this time the little red and white signs were doing their part in selling Burma-Shave and promoting highway
safety. "Hardly a driver
is now alive
who passed
on hills
at 75". Or, "Is he
lonesome
or
just blind
this guy who drives
so closely behind?" Burma-Shave. As roads grew wider and cars went faster
the little signs were located farther back from the right of way and much farther apart, so that the speed demons
of the time could read the copy at 55 or 60 mph.
By 1963 many of the great names that made automotive history were gone. Highways were improved and cars
were cruising at 70 mph, so the decision was made to discontinue the little signs of verse beside the road. The little
red and white signs that hadsold Burma-Shave and provided entertainment and promoted highway safety for 38 years
were no more. A set of signs was given to the Smithsonian, which reads: "Within this vale
of toil
and sin
your head grows bald
but not your chin".
Highway beautification calls for all signs to be removed, but I cannot help but wonder if the little red and
white signs were returned to highways and interstates, if they would again promote safety. One thing is sure -- they
would relieve monotony and bring some cheer into our driving time. Americans drive an average of one million miles
every minute of every day. "On curves ahead
remember sonny
that rabbit's foot
didn't save
the
bunny. It (The above is an excerpt from Rural and Urban Roads, by Hap Malott, Field Editor.)

The following is a report of services performed and projects accomplished on the Town's approximately 80 miles
of accepted roads:
Labor - This is the first year that the Highway Department had a Labor Account under which all personnel wages were
included. Previously, labor was charged to the account under which personnel worked. Just materials and hired
equipment are now charged under each budgetor article. The total labor expended for Fiscal 1979 was 391.48.
Chapter 90 Improvement - Mix-in-place surfaces were applied on Lancaster Avenue and Chase Road at a total cost
of $40,966.00.
General Highway Maintenance - Approximately 717 tonsof patch was used on various roads at a cost of $12,010.19.
Catch basins were cleaned, drainage work performed where necessary, brush was cut and chipped, roadside mowing,
sweeping, purchase of guard rail and small tools cost $91,374.46, of which $33, 134.67 was spent on 53,920 gallons
of asphalt under the Road Oil & Tar portion of this account.
Snow Removal - Wedid not have our usual amount of snowfall, but theicing conditions were far in excess of normal.
The cost of winter operations for hired equipment and materials was $67, 152.89.
Road Machinery Maintenance - This account entails maintenance and repairof all Highway equipment, purchase of
parts, plow and grader blades, chains, gas, oil, grease, diesel fuel, acetylene and oxygen and small tools. The
Highway Garage was burglarized resulting in the loss of all small tools, batteries, etc. which cost approximately
$4000.00 to replace. The total cost of maintaining our equipment was $45,573.17.
Town Barn Maintenance - Heat, light, telephone, janitorial and office supplies, postage, maintenance of office
equipment and special building maintenance cost $7,218.85.
Street Signs - Materials for fabricating street signs cost $759.51. A balance from 1978 of $1,111.22 was also spent
for this purpose.
Poison Ivy Spraying and Brush Cutting - Total cost was $1,900.00. The Highway Department is continually receiving
requests from Lunenburg residents to have this program expanded.
Special Accounts:
Installation of Drainage, Various Roads - Drainage wasinstalled in Savage Drive, Brown Drive, Northfield, Rolling
Acres, Burrage, West Townsend, Leominster, Prospect, Goodrich and Upland Avenue at a cost of $19,530.97, in-
cluding a balance from 1978 of $2,033.76.
Bridge Repairs - Repairs on old Route 225, Summer Street and Townsend Harbor Road cost $5,554.22.
Equipment - Under Revenue Sharing, a Dump Truck with Plow, a Truck Chassis and a Pick-up Truck were purchased
at a cost of $52,322.00.

Police Department
A 35% increase in stolen motor vehicles, the
Whalom Lakefront, continued fatal accidents along
Route 2A, serious injuries to a police officer in the
line of duty, contract negotiations and training of
officers were among the activities during 1979.
To implement provisions of a new police con-
tract, money was appropriated at a Special Town
Meeting in November for the appointment of two
additional police officers. These officers, Leo J.
Janssens, II and George E. Aho, were selected in
1979 with their appointments to become effective in
January 1980. Patrolman Aho is presently attending
Waltham Police Academy, and Patrolman Janssens
has completed his training in compliance with the
standards established by the Criminal Justice Train-
ing Council.
On October 1st, Patrolman John E. Baker was
struck by a hit and run motoriston Chase Roadwhile
executing a warrant. He is still convalescing from
his injuries and is expected to undergo further surgery
on his leg. Prior to his injuries, Patrolman Baker
participated in a specialized firearms training session
sponsored by the New England Association of Chiefs
Frederick B. Hobbs, Chief of Police
of Police in cooperation with the Federal Bureau of
Investigation, to qualify afirearms instructor.
Firearms training for local officers will be conducted by Patrolman Baker upon his return to duty. Credit should be
given to the Lunenburg Police Relief Association who purchased six 22 calibre target revolvers to be used in this
program.
Most of the motor vehicles stolen in Lunenburg were taken from driveways of private homes, and in many in-
stances, the keys had been left in the vehicles and the doors were unlocked. Residents are cautioned to be sure that
keys are taken from ignitions and that vehicles are locked, even on their own property.
Loitering, liquor, lighting and littering on the Lakefront all contribute to Leominster and Lunenburg's law en-
forcement problems. The so-called "bottle law" adopted at the last Annual Town Meeting has been an effective
tool in attempting to control some of the problems in this area. As a result of the newly formed Lake Whalom Lake-
front Association of Concerned Citizens, other control measures under consideration are: making the Lakefront one
way; restricting public use during certain hours; restricting parking and making a tow-away zone; and, ultimately,
closing the Lakefront off completely to motor vehicle traffic.
Generally speaking, there was an indication of crime reduction in Lunenburg during 1979. Statistics for the
year show that the breaks into private homes and businesses were down to 71 as opposed to 119 in 1978, with 65%
of all stolen property recovered. The dollar value of property stolen during 1979 did increase to $145, 412.00 of
which $94,740.00 was recovered. Larcenies remained about the same, at 158; but as previously mentioned, motor
vehicle thefts rose to 52 over the 33 stolen in 1978. Total calls for service this past year were 5,270 including 117
complaints for acts of vandalism, 15 assaults, two armed robberies, one bomb scare, and there were 81 persons taken
into protective custody as part of enforcement of the drinking laws.
Other activities within the Department included the investigation of sudden deaths by drowning and suicide,
responses to 370 alarms (false or otherwise), the issuance of 238 gun permits, the making of 672 house checks and
the serving of 239 summons.

===PAGE 29===
When writing the annual report of 1968, as Acting Chief of Police, the theme was "The time has come for
Lunenburg to take a good look at its Police Department". With the support of town officials and townspeople, this
has been done; and the Police Department has grown to provide 24 hour coverage, with a fully trained 11 man de-
partment and a clerk. We have new quarters and more sophisticated equipment such as a breathalyzer, radar and
radio equipment. Because of this, we are better trained and prepared to serve the public and to meet the needs
of the community.

Fire Department
In 1979 the Fire Department answered 446 calls
for assistance as follows: Rescue/Ambulance 251;
Brush Fires 48; Service Calls 39; Mutual Aid 19;
Accidents 18; Building Fires 17; Car Fires 14; Chim-
ney Fires 12; Needless Calls 6; Electrical Fires 4;
Furnace Fires 5; Ice Rescues 3; Lockouts 3; False
Alarms 3; Lawnmower Fire 1; Dump Fire 1; Drowning
1; Railroad Ties 1. Our fire and rescue/ambulance
calls remained about the same as in 1978 with only
slight changes in each category.
Our four fire trucks and one rescue/ambulance
are in good condition, except for the 1961 pumper
which should be rebuilt or replaced in 1981. The
life expectancy of a fire truck is about twenty years.
The cost of rebuilding is about half the cost of re-
placing.
During the year,bi-weekly meetings and train-
ing sessions were held. All firefighters are trained
as First Responders (CPR and First Aid). Also, there
are twenty men trained as Emergency Medical Tech-
nicians, This year seven firefighters attended the
fire training school in Fitzwilliam, New Hampshire.
The Men's Muster Team won second place in
the Massachusetts Fire Muster League. The Women's
Muster Team came in fourth in their division.
The Fire Chief would like to thank theofficers
and members of the Fire Department for their coop-
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
eration throughout the year.

Building Inspector
The Tri-Town Building Inspector wishes to take this opportunity again this year to thank Lunenburg residents
and Town officials for their cooperation and assistance throughout the year. The Building Inspector's office is lo-
cated in the Legion House at 27 Memorial Drive (Telephone: 582-4613) and the office hours are 8:00 A.M. to
4:00 P.M. Monday through Friday.
The records show that Lunenburg residents are doing a good job on the installation of wood stoves. "Keep up
the good work. 11 However, anyone installing wood stoves is required to obtain a permit from the Building Inspector
who can offer guidelines for a safe installation.
Building permits are required by the Massachusetts State Building Code as follows:
"SECTION 113.0 (Chapter 802 of the State Building Code)
It shall be unlawful to construct, reconstruct, alter,
repair, remove or demolish a structure; or to change the
use or occupancy of a building or structure; or to install
or alter any equipment for which provision is made or the
installation of which is regulated by this code without first
filing a written application with the building official and
obtaining the required permit therefor.'
During the period January 1, 1979 through December 31, 1979, $4,462.00 was received by the Town for
Building Permits and Certificates of Inspection issued.

Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
For the year ending December 31, 1979 a total of
153 inspections were completed. Also, 93 Plumbing Per-
mits and 88 Gas Permits were issued as follows: 56 Plumb-
ing Permits for new homes; 28 Plumbing Permits for remod-
eled homes; 9 Plumbing Permits for Commercial buildings;
2 Gas Permits for Commercial buildings; 86 Gas Permits
for various gas appliances.
All new homes require two inspections -- the first
is a water test which shall be applied to the drainage sys-
tem either in its entirety or in sections. The plumbing sys-
tem will be filled with water to a point of overflow. The
water shall be kept in the system or in the portion under
test for at least fifteen minutes before inspection starts.
The system shallbe free of any leaks before it will be ap-
proved. The final test and inspection, when the work in
a building is sufficiently advanced that the water may be
turned on to all the fixtures, the plumber doing such work,
shall notify the Inspector of Plumbing for afinal inspection.
These two tests must be made before a Certificate of Oc-
cupancy will be issued by the Building Inspector.
Thomas E. Mortis
For consumer protection, the repair of leaks in a faucet, valve or other working part of a plumbing fixture,
and the clearance of a stoppage, but not including the alteration, replacement or relocation of any faucet, valve,
or other working part of a plumbing fixture or device, water supply, sewer, drainage, leader, soil waste, vent or
similar piping or work affecting public health shall be deemed a minor repair for which no permit shall be required.
Installation of Gas Conversion Burners shall conform to American Standard Installation of Domestic Gas Conversion
Burners, Z21.8-1958 as amended to Z21.8-1965. This being the best possible method of conserving gas consumption.
No owner and/or tenant shall be deprived of heat when a gas conversion or gas design boiler unit is scheduled un-
til test and/or inspection arrangements have been assured by appointment with the Gas Inspector.

This year Mr. Wayne A. Balcom, who has been your
Inspector of Weights and Measures, resigned after seven
yearsof faithful service to the residentsof the Town. Your
newly appointed Inspector is Lauren W. Davies.
Scales, gasoline pumps andother commercially used
weighing and measuring devices are regularly tested for ac-
curacy once a year. This year, 57 scales and pumps were
tested and sealed, and 3 scales and pumpswere adjusted and
sealed.
Your Inspector extends his thanks to all the various
establishments that required his services for their full coop-
eration. Also, he wishes to thank Mr. Balcom for his val-
uable assistance in helping himwith the problems that came
up this past year, relative to the position of Inspector of
Lauren W. Davies

Dog Officer
The Dog Officer received and investigated five
hundred calls since taking over this position as of
July 1, 1979. At this time there are approximately
1,250 dogs licensed in Lunenburg - due in large
part to summer and fall notices being mailed out and
numerous telephone calls. Fine books have been
printed and citations are being issued at the time of
any violation of the Restraining By-law which was
votedon and passed at the 1979 Annual Town Meet-
ing. The fines are: $10. for 1st Offense and
$25.00 for each subsequent offense.
In seven instances, the Dog Officer, after due
investigation, found it necessary to ask that Perma-
nent Restraining Orders be placed on these dogs due
to the serious problems they had created in Town.
The Dog Officer in afew instances had to remove a
few dogs from the Town completely in order to insure
the safety of the citizens of Lunenburg.
Also, 118 stray dogs were picked up in Town
since July 1st. Four dogs were destroyed due to un-
treatable illnesses, 14 dogs were claimed by owners,
and the remaining 100 dogs were given away to per-
sons who can provide good homes for these animals.
The Dog Officer works diligently in finding the right
home for the right dog and truly believes that every
good dog deserves the chance for a good home and
that good old standby - Love.
There is still a continuing problem with dogs
Kathleen M. Comeau, Dog Officer
whose owners work and allow their dogs to run loose
and unattended. These dogs cause frequent aggrava-
tion to other residents, and in many ways become public nuisances. Often some of these dogs are picked up by the
Dog Officer severely injured and in some cases the dogs are found dead in the roadway.
Dogs running loose in the school yard have continued to be a very disturbing problem. It has now been decided
by School officials and the Dog Officer that no dogs will be allowed in the school yard at any time. The safety of
all our children is vitally important, and since this has become an almost uncontrollable problem, the rule will be
strictly enforced at all times. The Dog Officer checks the school grounds twice a day, seven days a week, and
school officials and personnel are also assisting in this matter. There will be no exceptions.
The Dog Officer wishes to remind all dog owners that should your dog become lost, there is no possible way for
anyone to know who the owner of the dog is unless the dog has proper tags on its collar. If all dogs were licensed
and wore identification tags, there would not be one lost dog in Town. Such a dog could be promptly returned to
its owner and tax dollarswould be saved as the Dog Officer would not have to spend many hours feeding and caring
for stray dogs. Should your dog's license tag become lost, you may purchase a duplicate at the Town Clerk's office
for ten cents. The State passed a law requiring dogowners to present a Rabies Shot Certificate to the Town Clerk's
office at the time of purchasing or renewing a dog license.
The Dog Officer wishes at this time to say thank you to the Board of Selectmen, Town Clerk and the Town Hall
staff for all the assistance given her, and also to the Police Department for their assistance. She also wishes to thank
the peopleof Lunenburg who have been very understanding and helpful. The Dog Officer is available at all times no
matter how small the problem may be and will do her best to help each and every resident in Town.

Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
At the annual reorganization meeting of the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District Com-
mittee, Mr. Burton E. Gould, Jr., of Winchendon, was reelected Chairman; Mr. Charles F. Valera, of Lunenburg,
was elected Vice Chairman; and Mr. Jospeh F. Gorman, Superintendent-Director, was elected Secretary. At a
later date, Mr. Stratos G. Dukakis was reelected Treasurer.
In July, the Committee selected Mr. & Mrs. Walter LaGrassa of 234 Cross Street, Gardner, Massachusetts,
to have a house built by the students in the Carpentry Department. Our Athletic Program continues to bring credit
upon the District with the Hockey Team winning its division and the first game of the playoffs. The first Annual
Field Day competitions at "Monty Tech" found the Electronic Department and the Data Processing Department tying
for first place requiring a one-event playoff which was won by Electronics.
Three new programs were added to the curriculum; a Building & Grounds Maintenance Program for students with
special needs; a Diesel Mechanic Program and a Plumbing Program. Again in 1979, the Annual MAVA Summer
Workshop for Vocational Teachers and Administrators was held at "Monty Tech". The curriculum for the School Year
1979-1980 was expanded to include more English, Civics and related shops with the teachers having the option of
using Laps or teaching in a traditional manner,
A Supervisor of Cooperative Education and Job Placement was added to the staff to ensure proper exposure of
the employing public to the capabilities and potential of our students. His responsibility is to place many qualified
students on Cooperative Education as possible. (This is where the student goes to work and gets paid on his shop
week.) In addition, his responsibility is finding employment for the graduating class.

Extension Service
We are pleased to report that 1979 was notonly a productive and interesting yearfor the two already existing
4-H Clubs, the Bridle Path 4-H Club under the leadershipof Mrs. Dixie Beck and Mrs. Nancy Womble, and the 4-H
Outdoor Adventure Clubwith Mrs. Joyce Downey and Mrs. Dot Connell as leaders. Also for the two newly organ-
ized groups, the Small Animal Club led by Ms. Cynthia Powell and the Cooking, Sewing and Knitting Club direc-
ted by Mrs. Agnes Price.
The Small Animal Club has fifteen members and meets weekly. The children concentrate on loving and car-
ing forsmall animals, mainly goats, dogs and cats. The children are not required to own an animal themselves. They
also are instructed on the proper ways to care and feed the animals.
The Cooking, Sewing and Knitting Club meets every other with membership as follows: Cooking Club has
twelve members who have prepared pumpkin bread, various sauces and cookies; Sewing Club has eight members who
- 32 -
===PAGE 35===
have made skirts and aprons; Knitting Club has three members andsome have made knit headbands. The Club leader
and members are looking forward to further expansion of this program next year.
The 4-H Outdoor Adventure Club has been in existence four years and now boasts a membership of ten. It in-
cludes: Maura Downey, Jean LeBlanc, Michelle Hood, Jennifer Hood, Kathy Connell, Bonnie Benedict, Pattie Ag-
sten, Beth Forget, Sara Moynihan and Kate Moynihan. These girls meet two Monday evenings a month and have had
the opportunity to explore and appreciate the historic Walden Pond area as well as the Jenks Woods Conservation area
of Lunenburg this year.

camperships, group riding lessons and our indoor/outdoor mounted meetings. Many thanks to all the generous spon-
sors who helped to make all of these extremely beneficial events possible; July - Awards Day was held in conjunc-
tion with a Swim Party at Henry and Ruth Anderson's home on Elmwood Road. A grand time was had by all. Several
members attended summer camp in Spencer. For those who could not attend camp, group riding lessons were arran-
ged; August - A Tooth Floating Clinicby Mr. Robinson was held. Judy Lorimer and Gail Greenlaw of East Pepper-

and second in State in his category. The title of his presentation was "Lifesavers" and concentrated on the proper
way to administer first aid to victims in three emergency situations: breathing, bleeding and poisoning. Our con-
gratulations to Joey! Further congratulations to Karin Anderson and Dean MacCracken who were selected to par-
ticipate at the Eastern States Exposition Horse Show and did a fine job representing our Club. Jennifer Roy, a new
Club member from Fitchburg, was an award winner at the State Horse Show. Congratulations, Jennifer!
We trust that the 1980 calendar year will be equally successful and further expanding.

Veterans' Services
A different type of report this year one of pro-
gress. We now have a caseload of five cases per month
compared to fifteen to twenty cases per month in the past,
and we are recovering more money from various sources
as explained below:
1. A cost-of-living increase (4.8%) in the overall bud-
get for all recipients and retroactive to July 1, 1979
under MGLA Chapter 115.
2. Veterans' Improved Pension (VIP).
3. Notice of intent (to each applicant when applying
for assistance).
4. Computer Program Sheets (reported quarterly).
5. Voluntary Assignments (sources of income).
6. Notice of Action (Agent's alternative).
Items #2 through #6 are briefly explained below:
Item #2 - Another new pensionplan has been passed and
has decreased our rolls because it offers more of an in-
come than other plans, i.e. single veterans under the old
plan received a pension of $197.00 monthly, but when
they apply under the Veterans' Improved Pension plan,
this changes to $295.83 which would put the veteran in
a status of self-support without our assistance. Not all
Alfred H. Gauthier, Veterans' Agent
persons eligible under this plan are referred to said plan
as they could lose some incomes. Therefore, they are
advised as to which plan would be most beneficial to them. After receipt of an application, the Veterans' Adminis-
tration sends a letter to the veteran indicating one of the following - Unadvantaged Election - Election Deferral -
Election Award. This office is then notified as to what procedure has been followed.
Item #3 - Notice of Intent - Each applicant is to receive a letter from this office in that effective January 1, 1979
we must provide the State Commissioner of Veterans' Services with a quarterly list of recipients on the rolls, and this
information is fedinto the State computer for cross-indexingwith all departments in the Commonwealth, so that wages
or benefits can be counter-checked against lists for accuracy of reported incomes, and appropriate action can be
taken against any person who is not entitled. This new law comes under MGLA Chapter 367, Acts of 1978.
Item #4 - Computer Program Sheets - In #3 above whereby we notify the recipient of our intent, in #4 we must sub-
mit a quarterly Computer Sheet to the State with all necessary data for a three month period to be fed into the com-
puter and balanced against reports submitted by Welfare, Employment Security, Social Security, etc. so that the
recipient cannot receive monies fraudulently.
Item #5 - Voluntary Assignments - We now have authority to regain any monies paid to a recipient who would get a
future income from any and all State departments, sick benefits, retirement, etc. This will assist US in helping others
when we get some of our expenses back.
Item #6 - Notice of Action - At the discretion of the Veterans' Agent, a recipient can be - Suspended Terminated -
Amended - Reduced Stipend. However, a letter indicating action taken must be forwarded to the recipient and the
State as to reasons for action taken, and further indicating that the recourse of the recipient is to write a letter over
his signature within thirty days to the Board of Appeals and said Board will notify the recipient and the Agent of the
date scheduled for a review of appeal.
With the combination of all of the above, we are able to weed out and help those that are in need, thus saving
the Town money and many hours of paperwork. At this time I wish to thank everyone who has assisted me during the
past year, especially while I was incapacitated, and trust that I may call on them during the coming year.

The 1980 Legislature has been deluged with bills relating to the improvement of municipal financial manage-
ment. Most of these bills imply a need to assist in managing, in varying degrees, the financial affairs of communi-
ties that find themselves in financial trouble. Some of the bills are intended to improve the borrowing abilities of
weaker cities and towns; others would tighten procedures relating to the setting of realistic tax rates; and still others
would establish municipal data banks, improve the financial reporting procedures, guard against spending deficits,
set performance standards, streamline cash management or mandate the reporting of financial performance data for
each city and town in the Commonwealth. All this could have been avoided ifmore City and Town Fathers had re-
alized that most municipal finance mismanagement can be solvedwith larger and betterpaid staffs - show me a town
with several year's back taxes and I'll show you a town with a shortage of tax collection clerks, or show me a town
that doesn't get maximum return on its investment dollars and I'll show you a town treasurer's office that is under-
staffed in number and quality. The size and quality of Lunenburg's Management Team has permitted this Town to
compare most favorably by whatever standard. This is a tribute to the farsightedness of those who "call the shots"
and raise the money, namely, the Finance Committee, Selectmen and, most importantly, the voters. Following is
a comparison of Lunenburg's Financial Performance Data for Fiscal Year 1975 through Fiscal Year 1979 based on
generally accepted criteria.
