Lines 1 through 4 show a speeding upof property tax collections, (98% in year of assessment), our goal in the
next year or so being 100% collections in year of assessment.
Line 5 demonstrates the fact that tax titles are quickly disposed of, as property is not allowed to remain in
limbo once a tax lien is put in place.
Lines 6 through 10 point out an extremely strong and favorable debt position with a debt ratio (debt to valua-
tion) under 1% and $79.15 debt per capita compared to 1960 debt ratio of 12.30% and debt per capita of $188.
Lines 11 through 13 reflect investment earnings totalling between one and two percent of revenue collected.
Although this is a yardstick that is being forced upon US and even though we score high marks comparatively speak-
ing, I fail to see the validity of the comparison as there are so many variants, i.e. cities and towns in a borrowing
position, fluctuating interest rates, etc. In any event in Fiscal Year 1979 wewere able to earn $116,047 on revenúe
cash invested; in calendar year 1979 - $145,798.09; and in the first six months of Fiscal Year 1980 (July through
December 1979) - $85,538.84.
Lines 14 through 16 reveal cash management procedures which permit nearly 100% of the Town's monies to be
invested at all times. This is accomplished through the use of zero-balance checking accounts, staggered warrant
payments, and several different investment vehicles including certificates of deposit, repurchase agreements and
pooled investments. A future goal is more emphasis on payment scheduling which will, of course, involve close co-
operation between departments and the finance officers.
- 38 -
===PAGE 41===
Line 17 makes US the envy of many another and town. We worked hard to build up and hold onto our Free
Cash and we become absolutely paranoid when the State or anyone else tries to pry it away from US. Some of the
advantages of a Healthy Free Cash Position are as follows: 1. Serves as a mechanism to stabilize the tax rate from
year to year; 2. Provides a source of funds to cover undetermined major expenditures, such as collective bargaining
contracts and emergency situations; 3. Reduces the need for borrowing in anticipation of revenue; 4. Positively affects
the interest rates the community must pay on bonds and notes.
Lines 18 and 19 would seem to say very little as they consist of a series of zeros. Actually, this says a great
deal as it means that we have not had to borrow in anticipation of revenue for the last six years. The amount saved
in interest on temporary loans added to the amount earned on revenue cash invested easily saves $2.00 to $3.00
yearly on the tax rate.
The comparison that follows interprets financial information which determines the amount of your real estate
tax. Preceding the chart is a short comment with regard to each item.
Item 1 reflects total appropriations for local costs. Note that the total increase (31%) over a four year period
in the amount of $1,446,720 represents average yearly increases of 7.7%. This compares to 9.29% average increase
during the same period in the Boston area Consumer Price Index for all wages, goods and services.
Items 2 through 4 Although State Assessments remain relatively constant, it can be noted that the County
Assessment has appreciably decreased. This is because Court Costs have been shifted to the State. Do you suppose
that it was an oversight that all amounts paid into the County Retirement System on account of court personnel are
now paid to the State, whereas all amounts paid out to court pensioners still come out of the County Retirement
System? I guess that explains a good part of the increase in Item 4 - Worcester County Retirement System.
Item 5 The Gross Amount to be Raised represents a four year increase of 28.28% or 7.07% per year.
Items 6 through 10 are the receipts which serve as offsets to amounts which would otherwise be raised by taxa-
tion. Revenue Sharing funds remain fairly constant, although there is no assurance that this program will continue
after Fiscal Year 1981. The $216,297 increase in State receipts is not, as they say, "all that it is cracked up to be"
if we consider that some of the largest spending increases (schools) result from State Mandated Programs, the promised
reimbursements for which seem to have shrunk. For instance, in Fiscal Year 1976 reimbursements under Chapter 70
for School damounted to 33% of the total school budget. In four short years this percentage has decreased to 28%.
Had wereceived 33% in Fiscal Year 1980, income from the State would be $207,736 greater and would have meant
a $3.00 decrease in the tax rate. Even the 33% is several hundreds of thousands of dollars less than afforded by the
original School Aid Formula, which was whittled and chipped away at until it was hardly recognizable and then
scrapped for something less acceptable.
In Item 8, the Local Receipts which reflect the greatest increases derive from the motor vehicle excise and
interest on invested funds. The Fiscal Year 1981 figure (based on calendar 1979 actual) is $773,933, an additional
$154,263. Surplus Revenue, Item 9, is a good example, aspointed out above, of how a Healthy Free Cash Position
can be used as a mechanism to stabilize the tax rate. Keep in mind that in Fiscal Year 1980 every $72,693 in sur-
plus revenue appropriated reduced the tax rate by $1.00. Other available local funds, Item 10, consist mostly of
unexpended appropriation balances which, of course, vary appreciably from year to year. The fact that this figure
has decreased may well be attributed to the fact that original appropriations are more carefully estimated, another
sign of a well-run town.
Items 11 and 12 Total Offsets to Taxation only increased 14.5% oranaverage of 3.6% peryear. Wereiterate
that, in spite of claims by the State to the contrary, the main reason this item did not hold its own is because the
State hasreduced our entitlement with regard to State Mandated Programs. It follows, therefore, that over the four
year period the net amount to be raised by taxation increased a total of 41%, or an average of 10.3% per year,
slightly higher than the Boston CPI of 9.29% for the same period.
Item 13 What does all this mean to the average Lunenburg Taxpayer with an assessed valuation of $31,000?
It means that because he has voted to keep the same level of services during the last four years, his tax bill has in-
creased $393 or an average of 8% per year. That is to say, HE IS GETTING HIS INFLATED DOLLARS WORTHI

===PAGE 78===
VITAL STATISTICS
Following are those records of Births, Marriages and Deaths that either occurred in our Town or records which were forwarded
to this Town from other municipalities in the State. Lunenburg residents are urged to notify the Town Clerk if in-State records have
not been received and recorded. If a Birth, Marriage or Death occurred outside the State, the same may be recorded in the Lunen-
burg Town Clerk's office if certified records are presented. In some cases, the Town Clerk's records are supplemented by records of
burials which appear on Page 14 of this report. In order for these records to also be included in the official Vital Statistics records,
the family of the deceased should present a certified copy of the death certificate to the Town Clerk.

COMMITTEE REPORT, SUCH ACTION TO BE EFFECTIVE JULY 1, 1979.

ARTICLE 7. To see if the will vote to appropriate a sum or sums of money for the acquisition by the Board of Selectmen
in behalf of the Town, by purchase or otherwise, including but not limited to taking by eminent domain, certain land in Lunenburg,
off Youngs for the purpose of establishing a new municipal refuse disposal area of the type commonly referred to as a sanitary
landfill, and authorize the Selectmen to do all things necessary in pursuance of such acquisition, and determine how the money shall
be provided, whether by taxation, transfer from available funds, borrowing or a combination of the foregoing and, if by borrowing,
to authorize the issue and sale of bonds or notes, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED, 392
VOTES HAVING BEEN CAST IN THE AFFIRMATIVE AND 152 IN THE NEGATIVE, THAT THE TOWN APPROPRIATE THE SUM OF
$123,000 FOR THE ACQUISITION BY THE BOARD OF SELECTMEN IN BEHALF OF THE TOWN, BY PURCHASE OR OTHERWISE,
INCLUDING BUT NOT LIMITED TO TAKING BY EMINENT DOMAIN, CERTAIN LAND IN LUNENBURG, OFF YOUNGS ROAD,
FOR THE PURPOSE OF ESTABLISHING A NEW MUNICIPAL REFUSE DISPOSAL AREA OF THE TYPE COMMONLY REFERRED TO
AS A SANITARY LANDFILL, AND AUTHORIZE THE SELECTMEN TO DO ALL THINGS NECESSARY IN PURSUANCE OF SUCH
ACQUISITION, AND THAT TO MEET SUCH APPROPRIATION $23,000 BE APPROPRIATED FROM SURPLUS REVENUE AND THAT
THE TREASURER WITH THE APPROVAL OF THE SELECTMEN BE AND HEREBY IS AUTHORIZED AND DIRECTED TO BORROW IN
THE AGGREGATE $100,000, AND ISSUE AND SELL BONDS OR NOTES OF THE TOWN THEREFOR, PAYABLE IN NOT MORE
THAN 10 YEARS, IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE PROVISIONS OF CHAPTER 44, SECTION 7 OF THE GENERAL LAWS.

ARTICLE 15. To see if the Town will amend its By-lawsby striking out Section 12 of Article Vas it presently appears and by
inserting in place thereof a new Section 12 as follows: "SEC. 12. No person shall drink any alcoholic beverage as defined in
Chapter 138, Section 1 of the General Laws of the State, or possess an opened container full or partially full of any alcoholic bev-
erage, while on, in, or upon any public way, upon any way to which the public has right of access, in any place to which members
of the public have access as invitees or licensees, in any park or playground, conservation area or recreation area, on private land
or place without consent of the owner or person in control thereof. Any person convicted of violating this ordinance shall be pun-
ished by a fine not exceeding TWO HUNDRED ($200.) DOLLARS for each offense.", or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANI-
MOUSLY.
ARTICLE 16. To see if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by striking out Section 1 of Article IX and inserting in place
thereof the following new section: "SEC.1. DOG RESTRAINT. No owner or keeper of any dog shall permit such dog, whether
licensedor unlicensed to become a public nuisance within the town of anytime. It shall be the duty of the Dog Officer to apprehend
any dog which he has reason to believe to be a public nuisance and to impound such dog in a suitable place or to order the owner
thereof to restrain such dog. Theowner of any dog impounded may reclaim such upon payment of the sum of ten ($10.00)* dollars
for the first twenty-four hour periodor any part thereof that the dog is held and ten ($10.00)* dollars for each additional twenty-four
(24) hours or any part thereof; provided, however, if the dog is not licensed that before release to any person a license, if such is
required by the Town, shall be secured. The sums collected pursuant to the provisions of this section shall be accounted for and
paid over to the Town Treasurer. Any dog which has been impounded and has not been redeemed by the owner within ten (10) days
- 85 -
===PAGE 88===
shall be disposed of as provided for by the laws of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. Any owner found in violation of any of the
provisions of this By-law shall be guiltyof a misdemeanor, and upon conviction thereof shall be punishable by a fine in accordance
with the following scheduleor any other schedule permitted by law: 1st offense - $10.00; 2nd offense - $25.00; 3rd and subsequent
offenses in any 12 month period $25.00. If the owner or keeperof a dog be a minor, the parent or guardian of such minor shall be
held liable for any violations of this By-low. The Dog Officer, members of the Police Department or whoever may be designated
by the Board of Selectmen, shall enforce the provisions of this By-law and shall attend to all complaints or other matters pertaining
to dogs in the Town with citation rights. Definitions. Dog. All animals of conine species, both male and female. Owner. Any
person or persons, firm, association, or corporation owning, keeping or harboring a dog as herein defined. Public Nuisance. Any
dog shall be deemed a public nuisance when attacking persons or domestic animals while such dog is on property other than that of
the dog owner; when destroying property; when on a public school grounds and not under restraint; when it persistently chases moving
vehicles; when it persistently and continuously barks or howls; when it is permitted to run at large and unrestrained on property other
than its owners; any unspayed female dog in season shall be deemed a public nuisance when not confined indoors or housed in a
veterinary hospital or registered kennel. Each time one of the above nuisances exist shall constitute a separate offense.", or act
anything thereon. VOTED. (*reduced from $10.00 to $2.00 by the Attorney General in order to conform with statutory regulations)

ARTICLE 22. To see if the Town will vote to establish, under the provisions of the Massachusetts General Laws Chapter 40,
Section 8D, an Historical Commission for the purposes and with the rights and duties provided by law, to be composedof six members,
appointed by the Board of Selectmen for the terms of three years except the initial appointment shall be two members forone year,
two members for two years and two members for three years. Purpose of a local Historical Commission- The local Historical Com-
mission is the agency responsible for identifying and recording the historic assets of its community and for developing and imple-
menting a program for their preservation. The commission cooperates with other municipal agencies such as the planning board and
conservation commission to insure that the goals of historic preservation are considered in planning the future development of the
community. The commission also coordinates the activities of local groups whose interests involve history or historic preservation,
such as local historical societies and private preservation organizations, serving as a liaison between these groups and the State,
and Federal agencies and preservation organizations, or act anything thereon. VOTED.

No-School Signals - 1979-80
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to schools.
The following schedule of No-School Signals will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS of two (2) blasts on the horn at the Central Fire
Station indicates no school at the following times for the
groups indicated:
6:30 AM Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM Grades Kindergarten through 5
10:00 AM PM Session, Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of Radio Stations WEIM and WFGL,
Fitchburg; WLMS, Leominster, as well as WTAG and WSRS (Stereo 96) in Worcester;and WBZ in Boston,
as promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone
calls as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center
or bus contractors might not be notified on time. Parents can help by not allowing their children
to make unnecessary calls. Your cooperation and understanding will be appreciated by all con-
cerned.
- GODSPELL -

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
WILLIAM C. ALLARD, Ed. D.
It is a privilege to submit my
Second Annual Report as Superintendent
of Schools in Lunenburg. This report
covers the year ending December 31,
1979.
As you read the reports of the
other administrators and principals,
you will receive a more detailed
accounting of the events that have
taken place in the schools. The pur-
pose of this report is to summarize
the total aspects of the Lunenburg
Public Schools.
This past year has been for me
personally a very rewarding year. It
has provided me with the opportunity
to be involved with the staff and
supportive personnel on a continuing
basis. I have been most pleased by
this opportunity because it has
afforded me the chance to be more in-
formed and knowledgeable about the
entire school system. Although we do
not face many of the problems that
large school systems are facing during
these difficult times, we are experi-
encing the difficulties of a declining
enrollment and the rising inflation of
goods and salaries. Much can be said
of the support that this community
gives to its schools but the greatest
contribution is made by the students
who attend our schools. They are very
dedicated and sincere learners. All
of us associated with them on a daily
basis realize their commitment and
perseverance to utilize the talents of
our staff to its fullest in order to
achieve their personal educational
goals, I congratulate them on their
fine achievements and thank their
parents for providing them with a
supportive environment.
- THE PAST YEAR -
The 1978-79 school year was the
first year under our present grade
structure. The overall evaluation of
the reorganization is that it has im-
BURTON E. GOODRICH, JR., Ed. D.
proved tremendously the quality of the
- Assistant Superintendent -
environment and curriculum of our
schools. Much praise and credit for
- 96 -
===PAGE 99===
the success of this grade reorganization must go to the Principals who had to provide the leader-
ship to the staff and prepare the setting and materials for their new students. We are very
grateful for their dedication and concern for a very difficult transition period.
At the Elementary School, Mr. Ralston and the staff have done an excellent job in making the
necessary curriculum changes with the introduction of the fifth grade. We are constantly striving
to improve our curriculum to better meet the needs of a talented student body. The staff at the
Elementary School are to be particularly congratulated on the implementation of the Friday mini-
course experience. This has brought to the students evidence of the talents of our staff along
with exposure to new knowledge. I also have been very impressed with the Primary School activi-
ties and the excellent environment that the staff has established for the students who attend
that facility. The enthusiasm and creativity that engulfs the students is a remarkable educa-
tional achievement.
At the Middle School, we have worked long and hard to achieve our educational objective
that is, the middle school environment should be an upward extension of the elementary school
philosophy, rather than a downward extension of the high school. Mr. Barney and his staff are to
be recognized for their efforts to accomplish this goal. They have done an excellent job towards
this effort. The constant evaluation and revision of the curriculum, the successful implementa-
tion of the after-school intramural program, the observance of the importance of the individual
student in the total environment are all testimony to the professionalism and talent of the
staff. We hope that the community realizes the accomplishments of the students and staff at the
Middle School and continue to support the philosophy and efforts of this program.
At the High School, we have had greater opportunity than ever before to restructure the
curriculum and environment. With the removal of the eighth graders, the administration and staff
have had for the first time the availability of space and staff to concentrate on a grade 9-12
student body. Mr. Carey and his staff have done an excellent job in the preparation of materials
and establishment of an environment conducive to the learning needs of these students. Evidence
of this preparation has been the introduction of new courses such as the computer, the installa-
tion of a foreign language laboratory, realignment of classrooms so that departments are now
collected in an area of the school, the introduction of new graduation requirements, the imple-
mentation of a new attendance policy and the outstanding maintenance of our buildings by the
custodial staff. All of these efforts have produced a learning environment that I feel are abso-
lute to a better quality program for our, students,
Last year's report indicated some goals that the school department would be working on during
this school year. In this portion of the report I will discuss our efforts to date on these
issues:
- MINIMUM COMPETENCY -
Through the efforts of a very large committee headed by Mr. John McCarthy, English Department
Head at the high school, we have placed ourselves in readiness for the implementation of this law
which takes affect during the 1980-81 school year. This committee has had representation of the
teaching staff, students, parents, school committee and community representatives. They have
worked long and hard to prepare this school system with criteria necessary to implement the Basic
Skills mandate as set forth by the State Department of Education. We are very indebted to this
committee and all of our staff for having us as prepared as we are at this point.
- K-12 CURRICULUM EVALUATION AND ARTICULATION -
As a school system we have initiated much discussion in the area of curriculum. Through the
efforts of Dr. Goodrich, Assistant Superintendent, and the principals, department heads, head
teachers, and staff we have made some major changes in curriculum such as changes in the math and
social studies at the Elementary School, math and writing programs at the Middle School, greater
introduction of semester courses at the High School and an overall look at the sequence of our
curriculum offerings. We feel strongly that our programs should meet the increasing demands
placed on our students for better writing, reading and mathematics skills necessary for better
assimilation into today's society. To this end we will continue to improve the quality of our
offerings and create the flexibility in our programs in order to be current with today's needs.
97 -
===PAGE 100===
- INSERVICE PROGRAMS -
During the past year, we have made a greater commitment to this area than in the past. In
cooperation with the School Committee and a committee of staff individuals headed by Ms, Debbie
Colello, we have offered to the staff the following in-service courses:
1. Introduction to Programming a Computer
2. Stress Management Workshop
3. Adaptive Physical Education Workshop
4. Outdoor Education on Town-Owned Land
5. Understanding and Managing Health Problems and Medical Emergencies in the Classroom
Along with these programs, we have contin-
ued to be involved with the Merrimack Education
Center, the New England School Development
Council, Fitchburg State College and other
organizations to provide our staff with greater
opportunities to expand their knowledge for
classroom use.
- FIVE YEAR PLAN -
During a major portion of this year, in
close cooperation with the School Committee, we
have been working on a Five-Year Plan for our
school facilities. This plan would include an
updating of all of our facilities in order to
meet impending handicap laws, greater concentra-
tion on energy conservation measures and im-
proving our buildings in order to improve and
expand our curricula offerings. As of this
writing, a sub-committee of the School Committee
and the Office of the Superintendent have
formalized the Plan and we will be submitting
the entire proposal to the School Building
Assistance Bureau and to the Town Planning
Committee in preparation of submitting the pro-
posal to the Town at a special town meeting.
We feel strongly that this type of planning now
will make sense because it will eliminate the
possibility of very large expenditures to this
town in any one given year.
Our 1979 HOMECOMING GRAND MARSHALL,
Ginny Welch (shown above with
her husband, Bob).
- DECLINING ENROLLMENT AND REALLOCATION OF STAFF -
During the past year we have worked very closely with the Principals and the Lunenburg Edu-
cation Association in an attempt to reflect the results of a declining enrollment on our staff.
Last year we eliminated three (3) full-time teaching positions, a teaching aide and made some
consolidations in other positions within the school system. This year we are projecting at least
the elimination of two and one-half (2½) positions. Along with these reductions it is encumbered
upon us to continually look at the possibility of reallocation of our staff. We therefore may
have to set our schedules at the Middle School and the High School on the same schedule so that
we might share our staff between these two schools more effectively than under the present sched-
ules. We are thankful for the School Committee's willingness to understand this problem and to
leave it to the Superintendent to make recommendations in this area.
- 98 -
===PAGE 101===
- FUTURE CONSIDERATIONS -
Some of the goals that I consider important for future concern are:
1. Develop a GIFTED AND TALENTED PROGRAM.
2. Develop a model for remediation within the context of the BASIC SKILLS PROGRAM.
3. Continue our efforts in updating our FACILITIES.
4. Seek the most qualified and capable replacements for our TWO RETIRING PRINCIPALS.
5. Increase the participation of the COMMUNITY in our schools.
6. Continue the restructuring of our CURRICULUM.
All of these goals and many more that I did not list require the concentrated efforts of the
entire community. All of us share one major concern.
that we can provide for our children the
highest quality educational program available, I urge you to maintain your financial and moral
support to this goal. As a society, we reap the results of these efforts in producing a well-
adjusted, well-educated citizen who can contribute his or her talents to solving some of its
problems.
- CONCLUSION -
In concluding this report, I would like to pay special tribute to the members of the School
Committee for their countless hours spent in preparation of material and formulation of policy This
community has produced an interested, concerned and highly educated citizen to be members of its
School Committee and as Superintendent it has been a pleasure to work with them. To my fellow
administrators who have shared the joys and sorrows of a complex organization, I thank them for
their patience and understanding. I wish to thank the entire staff in the system for making it
work day in and day out, making each day a productive one for our students. Special thanks go to
Mrs. "Ginny" Welch, who has both the knowledge and love for this system to make it work smoothly.
Also, to Dr. Burton Goodrich, Assistant Superintendent, who has done an outstanding job in chasing
down a myriad of details and yet has contributed many creative ideas and thoughts. Finally, to the
townspeople who have treated me personally with respect and understanding and who have indicated
their willingness to support our efforts. What we have in quality is a direct result of your
participation and support.
Report of High School Principal
- SCHOOL EVALUATION -
1.
Of significant importance this year was the invited visit of an evaluation team from the New
England Association of Schools and Colleges, of which association Lunenburg High School has
been an institutional member since 1954 and a fully accredited member school since 1959.
2. The New England Association of Schools and Colleges is the oldest of the six regional
accrediting agencies in the United States. Since its inception in 1885, the Association has
awarded membership and accreditation to high school and collegiate institutions in New
England who are voluntarily affiliated.
3.
Each institution in the Association must host an evaluation team (as a pre-condition to
accreditation) at least once every ten years.
- -99- -
===PAGE 102===
RICHARD J. CAREY
PHILIP M. CROUSE
- Principal -
- Assistant -
4.
The Executive Committee of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges, at its Decem-
ber 1979 meeting, approved the recommendation of the Commission on Public Schools that Lunen-
burg High School be awarded continued accreditation for five years through 1984, as a result
of the visiting evaluation team report.
5.
A visiting committee of twenty-three educational evaluators, selected on the basis of their
competence and knowledge in their fields, spent three and one-half days in Lunenburg, reviewed
staff self-evaluation reports, and met with administrators, teachers and students on April 29
- May 2, 1979 to determine if the school's offerings were consistent with the philosophy and
objectives of the high school, as previously established by the staff and school committee of
Lunenburg.
6.
The report of the findings of the visiting team as forwarded to the Commission on Public
Schools of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges included, in part:
a. The philosophy of the school reflects the needs, talents and aspirations of the
students of the community and the objectives seem to be attainable, although some
are not being completely met.
b. The Program of Studies is extensive and consistent with the school's philosophy
but needs more attention to non-college courses.
C. Student services are satisfactory.
d. The Media Center (Library/Audio-Visual) is generally adequate although there is
need for a larger book purchase plan for the school.
e. The quality of the school's staff constitutes one of the school's strongest
assets.
- 100 -
===PAGE 103===
6. Recordkeeping is well done but record storage problems will continue and can
easily be corrected within the school confines and organization.
g. The school plant is essentially sound and adequate although roof problems
must be solved as soon as possible. Drainage in the gym area and a few
safety problems should be addressed as soon as feasible.
h. It is the opinion of the Visiting Committee that School-Community relations
are excellent in that the school is well supported by the community but that
more communication between the two is needed.
i. Student-Faculty rapport is excellent but the visiting Committee appreciates
that there are those concerned about budgetary restrictions. It is felt
that these concerns can easily be overcome through dialogue and discussion
before decisions are made affecting staff, student body, facility, and the
community.
While the evaluation team concentrated effort during their three and one-half days at the
school, and in the community, they recognized that some of their findings may not be consistent
with the school's perception of its responsibilities.
Lunenburg High School, through its Principal, is required to submit a Two-Year Progress Report
to indicate the status of all recommendations made by the Visiting Committee. All recommendations
must be classified into one of five categories: Completed, In Progress, Planned for the Future,
Rejected, or No Action.
The Final Report commended the school and community in over one hundred categories but
recommended that consideration be given to correcting deficiencies in over two hundred categories,
- COMPUTER AT LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL -
A computer system has been installed in Lunenburg High
School consisting of a main frame of dedicated memory. two
pointer terminals, two video terminals and the capability of
being expanded in memory, printer and video,
The equipment will provide extensions to business skills
and studies in science and mathematics as well as services
currently provided by special teachers to special needs stu-
dents.
- ATHLETICS -
The Lunenburg High School football team experienced its
best season ever in terms of victories since the adoption of
the program nearly twenty years ago. The Blue Knights final
record of ten wins and one defeat was highlighted by a thrill-
ing come-from-behind triumph in the Division II championship
Super Bowl game held on neutral Grafton High football field.
Lunenburg defeated Southbridge High School by a score of
15-14 in a game that featured an excellent over-all team
effort.
The squad, coaching staff, cheerleaders, band, student
body and the community in general are to be congratulated for
their enthusiastic support.
- 101 -
===PAGE 104===
- ACADEMICS -
The Class of 1980 has received recognition for outstanding scholastic achievement this year
from the National Merit Scholarship Corporation. As a result of tests administered in 1979 to
over a million high school juniors in all fifty states, three of our students were recognized as
Semi-Finalists, placing them in the top half of one percent (0.5%) of all the senior class stu-
dents, public and private schools, within the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. Upon the school's
recommendation based upon four years of academic achievement, in all probability these students
will become Finalists. The students so honored for their scholastic application, interest, and
attitude are:
- MARY L. BARNEY -
- HAZEL J. LATHAM -
- JOANNE C. SANDBERG -
In addition, two other students recieved Letters of Commendation as a result of their scores
on the same battery of tests. This recognition placed these students among the top two percent
(2%) of all secondary school seniors throughout the country. These students so recognized from a
public high school of our size is extremely significant--HEIDI M. EDES and WENDY S. MALE. These
outstanding accomplishments bring great credit to the students themselves, the school staff, and
the community itself for providing the opportunity to reach these achievements.
- CONCLUSION -
I would like to thank my fellow administrators, especially my very capable assistant, Mr.
Philip Crouse, teachers, office staff, school committee members and other members of the non-teach-
ing staff for their dedicated support and outstanding service during the past year.
Report of Middle School Principal
FLOYD F. BARNEY
- Principal -
On September 5, 1979, the Middle
School opened with an enrollment of
479 students. Grade 6 had 147 stu-
dents, grade 7 had 164 students and
grade 8 had 168 students.
As this report is being
written, our present enrollment now
stands at 479 students. Although we
are faced with declining enrollment
in Lunenburg, the Middle School will
have a slight increase in student
enrollment for the 1980-81 school
year.
- CURRICULUM -
The Middle School made two major changes in its curriculum for the 1979-80 school year and
initiated a Guided Study Program.
Mathematics - The I. M. S. math program in grade 6 was replaced with the Holt Mathematics
series. We feel this was a change for the better because we use the Holt series
in grade 7 and 8 and this would insure more continuity at this age level.
- 102 -
===PAGE 105===
Social Studies - The Data Bank social studies
program in grade 6 was replaced
with a new text entitled "Cul-
tures" which we felt was more
suitable and again gave us more
stability and continuity to our
entire program. We still use
the Data Bank series as a
supplement to the social studies
program when it is pertinent to
the unit under study.
Guided Study Program - Under the direction of
Mrs. Lynn Fletcher, a reading
specialist, a Guided Study Pro-
gram was initiated. The pro-
gram is designed to teach basic
reading and study skills to
Middle School students needing
remedial instruction and
strengthening in these areas.
Students meet in small groups
with a reading specialist to
receive individualized tutoring.
The skills emphasized in each
child's program depend on his/
her individual needs, and in-
clude vocabulary development,
reading comprehension, word
recognition, and/or study
RICHARD J. GARVEY
skills.
- Administrative Assistant to Principal -
Students' content-area textbooks are the basic materials used in the Guided Study classes.
Reading lessons are prepared from these textbooks, resulting in improved reading ability in
general and in the specific reading skills needed for reading in the subject areas. Assistance
in preparing for tests, doing research reports, and keeping up with homework assignments is also
provided.

The Second Annual Poetry Contest
for all eighth grade students was
held recently at Turkey Hill Middle
School.

chosen from the combined English

selected by the judges. The winners
were chosen on the basis of projec-

poise.

placed third; Jimmy Richards, second;
and Chris Fors, first. Debbie

honorable mention.

Burton Goodrich, Dr. Rose Feinberg,
and Mrs. Lynn Fletcher.

- RETIREMENTS -
- Mrs. Barbara Patch retired from her Middle School teaching in June of 1979 after sixteen
years of dedicated service to the school system. Barbara taught many different disciplines
and many different grade levels during her tenure. We all wish her much happiness in the
years ahead.
- Mrs. Alma Kilpatrick is retiring on February 1, 1980 after fifteen years of dedicated ser-
vice to the school system. It is with regret that we accept her resignation because of her
teaching ability and understanding of children. Alma is moving to Maine because of her
love for the ocean. May her retirement years be filled with happiness.
- Mrs. Anne Styrna retired in June of 1979 after serving as a clerk/typist at the Middle
School for eight years and three and one-half months. Anne was a very jovial person with
students and faculty and always willing to help people with their problems. We all extend
our thanks for her dedicated service and wish her the best in the years ahead.
- CONCLUSION -
I would like to express my appreciation to Mr. Garvey and the staff of the Middle School for
their help and commitment to quality education and their willingness to provide the time andeffort
to enhance the quality of the total school program.
Report of Elementary School Principal
PAUL E. RALSTON
ROBERT FINNERON
- Principal -

- 105 - .
===PAGE 108===
The opening of school enrollment for 1979-80 totaled 756 pupils in grades K-5. This repre-
sented a substantial decrease from our population of 804 students at the start of the previous
year. This decline is most noticeable in the primary grades, and town census figures indicate a
continued enrollment decrease. Due to this trend the elementary school will be eliminating one
class each at the kindergarten, first, and second grade levels for the next school year.
The following is a student count at each grade level as of February 1, 1980:
Kindergarten
96
Grade one
102
Grade two
144
Grade three
138
Grade four.
128
Grade five
158
- PERSONNEL CHANGES -
Mrs. Isabelle Karl retired from a third grade teaching position in June 1979. Mrs. Karl had
been teaching at the third grade level for thirteen years in the Lunenburg Public Schools. Her
kindness and conscientiousness will be remembered by students and teachers alike.
Mrs. Edith Denney was hired to the position of elementary guidance counselor. Mrs. Denney
attended Wheaton College and the University of North Carolina. Her graduate study was done at
Fitchburg State College and Boston University. Mrs. Denney's previous work experience included
teaching in a Title I position in Bolton, adoption case work and counselor experience at the Robert
Kennedy Action Corps in Lancaster.
Mrs. Deborah Hoffman-Scoggins was appointed to a newly created position of half-time school
adjustment counselor. Mrs. Scoggins is a graduate of Oakland University at Rochester, Michigan
and holds two graduate degrees from American International College in Springfield, Massachusetts.
Previous work experience included psychological therapy with the W. R. A. P. Center in Westfield,
Massachusetts.
Janet Milliken has been added to the reading staff at the Passios School to replace Mrs.
Celona, who resigned in June of 1979. Mrs. Milliken's educational background includes a B. S.
degree from the University of Tampa, Florida in 1963 and an M. S. from the University of Maine,
Orono in 1966 with a major in reading. She has taught first grade for three years, Title I summer
reading, and has clinical experience in a private remedial clinic for five years,

- READING OLYMPICS -
Students in grades four and five joined the March of Dimes Reading Olympics Program this past
fall. Seventy-seven students volunteered to read books for financial pledges. These students
raised over $1100. for the Worcester County March of Dimes, The program, which mutually benefits
both student and charity, will become an annual event for elementary students.
- CONCLUSION -
I would like to express my appreciation to the entire staff of the Lunenburg Primary School
and T. C. Passios Elementary School, for the fine work done on behalf of the students. I would
especially like to acknowledge the continued support and dedication of Mr. Robert Finneron, Mrs.
Shirlie Ruggles, and Mrs. Vera McCarthy.
Finally, I wish to thank the many organizations and parents who contribute to the success of
our school's activities. Without this type of volunteer effort we could not provide the many
extras which enhance the quality of our school program.
- REPORT OF DIRECTOR OF READING & LANGUAGE ARTS -
A major focus in 1979 was on implementing
and further developing the program changes intro-
duced the previous year and explained in the 1978
Town Report: the Elementary 90-minute Language
Arts Block, the Seventh Grade Communications
Reading Program and the Eighth Grade Life Skills
Reading Program. The continuation of these pro-
grams is based on the generally positive response
of both teachers and students at the levels where
these new approaches have been implemented.
writing has received renewed emphasis this
past year. Building on research and the under-
standing that writing is improved by giving chil-
dren frequent opportunities to write with feed-
back, more time has been spent during language
arts (Elementary School) and English (Middle
School) on writing. There are many approaches to
teaching writing and reinforcing writing skills.
We have used a variety of activities and programs
to involve children in writing.
ROSE M. FEINBERG, Ed. D.
There is some concern that more attention to
writing results in less teaching of reading. It
should be understood that there are many ways to teach reading in addition to using a basic text-
book. One valuable way to teach and reinforce reading is through writing. Since reading involves
getting meaning from print, there are many opportunities to teach, enrich and test reading by
having children write (not just "creatively" but factually and in response to questions).
- LANGUAGE ARTS AND THE BASIC SKILLS IMPROVEMENT POLICY -
The Massachusetts Department of Education has produced a Basic Skills Improvement Policy
which affects all school systems in the Commonwealth. The Policy includes the major language arts
headings (reading, writing, listening, speaking) and mathematics. Although program plans for
reading and mathematics are not required until August 1980, with plans for listening and speaking
not required until August 1981, members of the Lunenburg School System spent much time in 1979 on
aspects of the Basic Skills Improvement Policy.
- 109 -
===PAGE 112===
A Committee on Competency was formed including teachers, parents and students, with John
McCarthy, English Department Head, as chairman. Grade levels for testing students were established
using State Department Guidelines and decisions were made about procedures for implementing the
regulations.
Reading Competencies will be tested in grades two, four and eight. Grades two and four will
be tested using the Stanford Achievement Tests (testing used for a number of years), and eighth
grade will be tested by the Massachusetts Assessment of Basic Skills - Reading. Other informal
measures will be used in addition to the Achievement Tests
writing will be tested in grades three, five and eight formally and in other grades
informally. Samples of writing will be used, as well as teacher judgment of students' writing
skills in work through the year.
The Lunenburg School System will set its own standards for what is considered "basic compe-
tency." There are no standards which are state-wide; and each community must establish its own.
The objectives and standards will be available to the public when completed.

- CONCLUSION -
The year 1979 has been described by some as a "Back to Basics" year. In Lunenburg, teaching
of basic skills in language arts has been part of the curriculum for many years. It is important
to realize that children learn in many ways, and that what may appear to be a "game" can be a way
of teaching a skill which will be remembered longer by the child because it involves "doing." The
programs in reading and the other language arts use many different materials and approaches to
teach the basic skills needed. Our goal, however, is to go beyond programs which address only
basic skills--to provide an education which encourages children to develop their imaginations,
expands children's thinking skills and builds the desire to learn more.
- 110 -
===PAGE 113===
Report of Special Needs Director
FLOYD BENITZ
"In the light of other policy of the Commonwealth
to provide an adequate, publicly supported edu-
cation to every child resident therein, it is
the purpose of this act to provide for a flexi-
ble and uniform system of special education pro-
gram opportunities for all children requiring
special education."
(Regulations 766, Massachusetts Department of
Education, September 1978).
Regular class teachers are to be commended on their
instruction of many children with special problems. Though
these children receive attention from support personnel such
as special needs teachers and counselors, the major instruc-
tion time remains with regular education teachers.
- EMPLOYING AIDES -
It has become necessary to employ additional aides to assist with the instruction of a few
severely involved children. Prior to the implementation of Chapter 766, these children would not
have received the benefits of regular school placement. Formerly, the children would have been
transported to other cities and, in some instances, live away from home and family. Because the
students require such individual attention, regular class teachers and special needs teachers need
aides when such a child is assigned to the class. We now have some children who are integrated in
regular classes largely through the excellent work of the aide.
- SCREENING FIRST GRADERS -
The motor (coordination) training program for young children is available in regular educa-
tion. Children who would gain from this service are identified through first grade screening.
This same screening enables the faculty to bring services to children in the areas of speech,
counseling, language and perceptual abilities at an early age.
- COUNSELING FOR SPECIAL NEEDS -
The major advance of this year is in the employment of special needs adjustment counselors.
The regular guidance counselors work with most of the adjustment problems of childhood and adoles-
cence. The adjustment counselor receives referrals from guidance counselors and other school per-
sonnel wherein the needs are more severe. Lois Davenport and Deborah Hoffman-Scoggins make home
visits, contact community resources for the child and family and sponsor parent education groups
with weekly evening meetings to assist parents in coping. Their work has brought an important new
dimension to the mental health and school success of selected children. Indeed, the Lunenburg
children and parents are fortunate to have the benefit of the training and experience of these two
new faculty members. Commendation goes to the School Committee for their sensitivity to the rela-
tion of physical and mental health to children's success in school.
- COOPERATING IN PLACEMENTS -
Lunenburg has cooperative arrangements with neighboring towns wherein a child with a diffi-
culty may require grouping with peers of similar age and development. Similarly, the Special Needs
Educational Collaborative continues to enroll children with low-incidence problems beginning as
- 111 -
===PAGE 114===
young as age three years. Some students in later adolescence enroll in the Fitchburg-Leominster
Work Activity Center for training and employment.
- REMOVING STRUCTURAL BARRIERS -
In compliance with federal and state laws, some architectural barriers have been removed from
the schools. Adaptations have been made in lavatories and entrances to buildings. Outside areas
have been marked for parking by the handicapped. Bob Okerman and the buildings and grounds staff
have done a fine job with the modifications in buildings.
- DESIGNING CURRICULUM ALTERNATIVES -
Some special needs students currently become involved in Project Enhance as early as seventh
grade. The project has objectives of a pre-vocational nature. This involves the development of
good work habits, following directions and getting along well with others. Students who want a
vocational education enroll in the Montachusett Regional Vocational/Technical High School or
similar facility.
The staff of Project Greenthumb continue the excellent work with students interested in
horticulture and the larger work of fields and streams. A computer has been installed in the
high school for instructional purposes. Selected special needs staff members and students are
learning to use the equipment.
- CONCLUDING STATEMENTS -
It is parents and faculty working cooperatively in the interest of the child that results in
quality education. We report to parents on children's progress four times a year. In addition
to those scheduled dates that may stimulate parent conferences, I urge parents to call or visit
the teacher, principal or myself at the beginning of any concern.
The thoughtful and dedicated work of all the special needs aides, tutors, counselors and
teachers is greatly appreciated. The efforts of Evaluation Team Chairpersons and regular teachers
have been acknowledged by a number of parents. The high calibre of services of teachers of our
children in out-of-district placements and in home tutoring is gratefully recognized. Finally,
the assistance of Beth Lawrence, secretary, and Yasmin Many, work/study student, support the many
services to children.
School Nursing Report
JEAN E. HOBBS, R. N.
- Coordinating Nurse -
Services provided by the Lunenburg school health program begin with three and four year old
screening, includes the assessment of health status of entering kindergarten students, routine
screening testing for all students, involvement in Chapter 766, and progresses through health
appraisals, counseling, self-care education, accident and illness prevention, and appropriate
referrals for care.
Three school nurses carry out the program in the schools. Jean E. Hobbs, R. N., is coord-
inating nurse and full-time nurse at the Primary and Elementary Schools; Marie B. Richards,
R. N., is part-time nurse for the Middle School; and Jean M. Larkin, R. N., is full-time nurse
at the High School.
To enable them to be more effective in the area of health promotion and prevention of ill-
ness, all three nurses participated in a twelve-session lecture and seminar series on Preventa-
tive Medicine, co-sponsored by the Massachusetts Medical Society Auxiliary and Harvard Univer-
sity Extension Program. Subject areas included epilepsy, sleep disorders, juvenile arthritis,
common skin problems, headaches, carcinogens, and use of generic drugs.
- 112 -
===PAGE 115===
A seven-session in-service for some twenty-six staff members is being presented and coordi-
nated by the school nurses. The program covers such topics as the teacher's role as a health
observer, Lunenburg's health policies and program, emergency and obstructed airway procedures, a
demonstration of CPR, hyperactivity, allergies, hearing and emotional problems. Participants, in
addition to the nurses, are Fire Chief James P. Deming, Dr. Gary Asher, Dr. Geraldine Feldman, Dr.
Franklin Holzer, Guidance Director Meredith Bissell, and Terri Chipman, psychiatric nurse thera-
pist.
(RIGHT) Jean Larkin, R. N., and
Fire chief James Deming,
are demonstrating CPR
as part of the current
health education in-
service course for staff.
Immunization monitoring continues, with an emphasis on upgrading the immunization status of
transfer students. The entering of foreign-born students necessitated the adoption of criteria
for entry that requires a negative tuberculosis report, and proof of immunization. An immunization
clinic is held once a year in conjunction with Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. With the
approval of school physicians, required immunizations or tuberculosis testing are available at the
school for transfer students at any time during the year.
Also carried out in conjunction with Nashoba Boards of Health is the dental program. The
hygienst offers cleaning and fluoride treatments for students in grades two, five and eight;
examinations for students in kindergarten and grade three, and dental health programs for grades
one and four.
Tuberculosis testing for certification is provided for school personnel, bus drivers, and
substitutes. Other staff health services provided are yearly blood pressure screening and flu
shots, and blood pressure monitoring on request, as well as being available for health consulta-
tion and questions.
The regular student screening programs--vision, hearing, and scoliosis--is being expanded
this year to include blood pressure screening for students in grades six and eight.
The school nursing staff serve as members of the safety and health education curriculum
committees, and not only serve as resource people for teachers presenting health education seg-
ments in the classroom, but also actively participate in the family living, and first aid classes
at the elementary and high schools, and instruction of CPR on the high school level. Also, the
nurse at the high school serves as a counselor for students with health-related problems. All of
the nurses work closely with the guidance and adjustment counselors in providing services for stu-
dents.
The nurse involvement in Chapter 766/Special Needs, includes home visits to gather birth and
developmental history for students undergoing core evaluations, serving as a liaison with private
physicians, and as the medical representative on the core evaluation team.
This year, Elizabeth Lambert, R. N., is doing a Practicum in School Nursing as her final
semester toward a bachelor's degree from Fitchburg State College. Mrs. Lambert is a graduate of
Burbank Hospital School of Nursing, and also works part-time on Burbank's Intensive Care Unit.
- 113 -
===PAGE 116===
In addition to the three school nurses, Lunenburg's school health staff includes Dr. Joel
Neuschatz, who is responsible for sports physicals; Dr. Gary Asher, who provides coverage for
physicals for younger students and serves as a consultant to the nurses and health educator; and
Dr. Felix Heimberg, who provides medical coverage at football games.

- RETIREMENTS DURING 1979 -
ISABELLE L. KARL
ALMA M. KILPATRICK
BARBARA H. PATCH
ANITA E. FOREST - Anita served as a cafeteria substitute worker from May 1964 until November 1964
when she was hired as a permanent general worker to replace someone who was out ill. In
September 1965, she became a member of the high school cafeteria staff. when the Middle
School was opened and the Elementary School cafeteria reactivated, she trans ferred in March
of 1969 to the Elementary School to serve as its Baker. She was appointed as the Cook/Manager
at the Middle School in January 1974 and remained in this position until she resigned on
July 13, 1979 with retirement effective November 8, 1979. Our congratulations go to Anita for
the dedicated service she displayed over her fourteen years as a member of our food service
department. We wish her many happy and healthy years of retirement.
DOROTHY A. HILLMAN - Dorothy began as a part-time teacher aide in the learning disabilities pro-
gram at the Elementary School on January 4, 1971, During her time with us she assumed the
responsibility for teaching all kindergarten physical education classes as well as working
with individuals and small groups as a motor training aide. Her knowledge in this area plus
her outstanding way with children produced an excellent program. She gave eight years of
dedicated and conscientious service to Lunenburg students who were privileged to be in her
program. Dorothy retired as of June 18, 1979.
ISABELLE L. KARL - In September 1966, Isabelle was appointed as a grade 4 teacher in the Elemen-
tary Annex. The following year she trans ferred to teach a grade 3 class. After 13 years of
dedicated service to her students, Isabelle submitted her intention to retire as of June 18,
1979. May she enjoy her retirement years to the utmost.
ALMA M. KILPATRICK - On January 1, 1963, Alma began as a teacher of grade 2 in the Elementary
School to replace a teacher who had resigned. She remained on the staff until June 1964 when
she resigned for family reasons. However, she returned to teaching again in September 1965
until September 1976 when she requested a leave-of-absence. In September 1977 she returned
as a 2/7ths reading teacher at the Middle School. Her service spanned fifteen years and was
one of distinction and dedication. On February 1, 1980, she retired to move to the coast of
Maine. Our best wishes go to Alma for many enjoyable and healthy years of retirement.
BARBARA H. PATCH + Barbara began in the Elementary School as a teacher of grade 5 in September
1962. In all, Barbara contributed 17 years of outstanding service to our schools. Effective
June 18, 1979, she retired so that she and her husband could do some traveling. Our sincere
thanks to Barbara and our best wishes for the years ahead.
- 117 -
===PAGE 120===
GEORGE W. SHOSEY, JR. - George served as a custodian in the Middle School from April 7, 1975 until
January 8, 1980 when he retired. He performed his duties cooperatively and with dependability.
Our wishes go to him for a healthy and happy retirement.
ANNE M. STYRNA - Anne served as a part-time Senior Clerk/Typist in the Middle School from May 17,
1971 until June 29, 1979. A warm, friendly person and enthusiasm for life, Anne will be
missed by all who had the pleasure of knowing and working with her over her eight years of
service. May she enjoy many happy and relaxing years ahead. Don't ever lose that smile,
Anne!
WILLIAM B. WELTON - On June 28, 1971, Bill came to work as a custodian in the High School. He was
appointed as Groundsman for the Lunenburg Public Schools on April 17, 1972 and served in this
capacity until his retirement on June 8, 1979. Over the eight years Bill was with us he
always greeted everyone with a smile, a pleasant word and had boundless energy for the tasks
assigned. All of us at the Brooks House will miss Bill who looked after our building as well.
The children at the Elementary and Primary Schools will also miss "Santa" Bill as well. We
wish him the very best in the years ahead.
